FRAUD DETECTION
According to one estimate, worldwide fraud amounts to more than five trillion dollars, which
is discussed in the following link:
https://www.crowe.com/global/news/fraud-costs-the-global-economy-over-us$5-trillion
Earlier sections in this chapter discussed how outliers differ from inliers in terms of their
value, frequency, location, or some combination. On the other hand, inliers are common occur-
rences, which is to say, there is nothing unusual about the values for inliers. An outlier draws
attention to the possibility of fraud but does not necessarily indicate that fraud has occurred.
An anomaly is also an outlier that is a more serious type of outlier: there is a greater chance
that this type of outlier is also fraud. By way of analogy, consider a traffic light consisting of green
(go), yellow (caution), and red (stop). An outlier can be in any of the following ranges:
• between green and red
• between green and yellow
• between yellow and red
As such, there are different levels of caution involved with anomalies and outliers. Specifically,
an anomaly belongs to the third category, whereas a “benign” outlier (which is not an anomaly)
is in the second category. Moreover, the collection of all types of outliers is in the first category.
With the preceding points in mind, here is a short list of various types of fraud:
• credit card fraud
• payroll fraud
• insurance fraud
Although there is no single method for always determining fraud, there are some techniques
for detecting potentially fraudulent transactions. For example, if you encounter a suspicious
event for a customer, calculate the following values for that customer:
Outlier and Anomaly Detection • 49
• total purchase amount for this day
• number of transactions for this day
• time of day for each transaction
• number of locations
• addresses of those locations
Now compare the values in the preceding list with the customer daily transaction patterns to
see if there is a likely case of fraud. In case you’re interested, the following link contains a list of
forty-one types of fraud, along with techniques for fraud prevention:
https://www.i-sight.com/resources/41-types-of-fraud-and-how-to-detect-and-prevent-them/
TECHNIQUES FOR ANOMALY DETECTION
In simplified terms, an anomaly is an outlier that has a more serious nature, such as credit
card fraud, insurance fraud, or payroll fraud. As a simple example of a potential anomaly, sup-
pose that someone who lives in California suddenly makes a credit card purchase in New York.
If the person is on vacation (or a business trip), then the purchase is an outlier (it’s “outside” the
typical purchasing pattern), but it’s not an issue. However, if that person was in California when
the credit card purchase was made, then it’s most likely to be credit card fraud, as well as an
anomaly.
Unfortunately, there is no simple way to decide how to deal with anomalies and outliers in
a dataset. Although you can drop rows that contain outliers, keep in mind that doing so might
deprive the dataset—and therefore the trained model—of valuable information. You can try
modifying the data values (described later), but again, this might lead to erroneous inferences
in the trained model.
Another possibility is to train a model with the dataset that contains anomalies and outliers,
and then train a model with a dataset from which the anomalies and outliers have been removed.
Compare the two results and see if you can infer anything meaningful regarding the anomalies
and outliers. With the preceding points in mind, here is a list of various techniques and algo-
rithms for anomaly detection:
• LOF
• HBOS
• PyOD
• Numeric Outlier (IQR)
• z-score
• DBSCAN
• isolation forest
The local outlier factor (LOF) technique is an unsupervised technique that calculates a local
anomaly score via the kNN (k nearest neighbor) algorithm. LOF is a density-based algorithm
that is well-suited for finding local anomalies. Documentation and short code samples that use
LOF are here:
https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.neighbors.LocalOutlierFactor.html
50 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
https://towardsdatascience.com/outlier-detection-theory-visualizations-and-code-
a4fd39de540c
Anomaly detection with histogram-based outlier score (HBOS) is an algorithm that con-
structs a histogram for a given random variable. Moreover, the histogram elements are normal-
ized so that their height is between 0 and 1. The HBOS value is determined by calculating the
sum of the logarithm of the outlier score for all the features, and you can find the formula here:
https://www.dfki.de/fileadmin/user_upload/import/6431_HBOS-poster.pdf
Python outlier detection (PyOD) is another open-source -based library for anomaly
Python
detection. PyOD actually computes anomaly detection scores for more than twenty anomaly
detection algorithms (e.g., AutoEncoder, kNN, and PCA, among others). The anomaly scores
are displayed in a tabular format so that you can quickly discern which algorithms have the best
performance for a given dataset.
Numeric outlier is straightforward outlier detection technique that operates in a feature
space consisting of a single dimension. This algorithm uses the IQR method to calculate the Q1
value and the Q3 value, which are the first and third quartile values, respectively. Any value that
is outside the interval [Q1,Q3] is considered an outlier.
Z-score is a technique that uses a Gaussian distribution as the basis for the distribution of the
data in a dataset, and the outliers are simply the points that lie in either extreme (“tail”) of the
distribution. A threshold value—often the value 3—is used to compare with the normalized data
values, and those whose value is greater than 3 (or less than -3) are treated as outliers.
Density-based spatial clustering of applications with noise (DBSCAN) is a clustering-based
technique for identifying outliers that classifies data points in three different groups: core points,
border points or noise points, which are determined based on whether or not they have more
than or fewer than MinPoints in a neighborhood of radius epsilon. Note that MinPoints and
epsilon are hyperparameters in this algorithm, as well as the chosen distance measure. A code
sample with DBSCAN is accessible here:
https://donernesto.github.io/blog/outlier-detection-with-dbscan/
An isolation forest (useful for large datasets) calculates an isolation number for each data
point, which is the number of “cuts” that are required to “detach” a point from all other points
in the dataset. For example, leaf nodes in a tree have an isolation number equal to 1 because
their only connection is the edge to their respective parent. An example of an isolation forest is
accessible here: https://quantdare.com/isolation-forest-algorithm
SUMMARY
This chapter started with an explanation of outliers, which comprise a superset of anomalies
(the latter are more significant and more problematic). You also learned about fraud detection,
which includes forty-one different types of fraud. In addition, you learned about various algo-
rithms for detecting and removing outliers, such as LOF, HBOS, PyOD, z-score, DBSCAN,
and isolation forest.

WHAT IS DATA CLEANING?
Data cleaning, also called data cleansing, is the task of ensuring that the contents of a dataset
are complete, correct, and typically without duplicates. Hence, the focus of data cleaning is on
individual files instead of combining or transforming data from two or more files. Data cleaning
52 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
is often performed before any data transformation is performed. In some cases, data cleaning
must also be performed after a data transformation.
For example, suppose that a file contains employee-related data and a table also
CSV MySQL
contains employee-related data, both of which have been cleansed of inconsistencies and dupli-
cates have been removed. However, after exporting the table data to a file that is merged
CSV
with the first file, it’s possible that there are duplicates that must then be removed.
CSV
Incidentally, there are several techniques for determining the values to replace empty fields,
and the choice of techniques can range from making intuitively obvious changes to more subtle
factors. Sometimes you can specify the mean or the median for missing values, but in other cases
you need a more sophisticated technique. For example, suppose that a dataset with 1,000 rows
consists of two types of patients: those who are healthy (the majority) as well as patients who have
cancer. Obviously you want the number of sick patients to be as low as possible, which means
that the dataset is fundamentally imbalanced.
Unfortunately, machine learning algorithms can produce inaccurate results with imbalanced
datasets. Moreover, generating synthetic data whose feature values are based on the mean or the
median is probably risky. A better technique is called , which generates data values that
SMOTE
are close to values that appear in rows of the original dataset.
As another example, the format for dates, currency, and decimal numbers varies among
different countries. Examples of date formats include , , and
YYYY/MM/DD MM/DD/YYYY
(as well as other possible date formats). Incidentally, is an ISO stand-
DD/MM/YYYY YYYY/MM/DD
ard for numeric dates.
Number formats involve a comma “,” for the thousands position and a decimal “.” for deci-
mal values in the United States (e.g., $1,234.56), whereas Europe uses the opposite order for
numbers (e.g., 1.234,56). Depending on the dataset in question, data cleaning can also involve
working with an assortment of dates, currencies, and decimal numbers to ensure that all values
have the same format.
Moreover, if two files contain different date formats and you need to create a single
CSV CSV
file that is based on the date columns, then there will be some type of conversion process that
could be one of the following:
• convert the first date format to the second date format, or
• convert the second date format to the first date format, or
• convert both date formats to a third date format.
In the case of financial data, you are likely to also encounter different currencies, which
involves a conversion rate between a pair of currencies. Since currency conversion rates fluctu-
ate, you need to decide the exchange rate to use for the data, which can be:
• the exchange rate during the date that the files were generated
CSV
• the current currency exchange rate
• some other mechanism
Data Cleaning for Personal Titles
Sometimes a “brute force” solution is also the simplest solution, particularly when strings are
involved. For example, a person’s title can be misspelled in myriad ways that involve many pesky
Data Cleaning Tasks • 53
little variations, so it’s good to know how to perform this task in a manner that’s simple, intuitive,
and easy to extend with additional cases. For example, consider how you would replace each
string in the following list with either before you look at the following
"Mr","Ms", or "Mrs"
potential solution:
titles = ['mr.','MR','MR.','mister','Mister','Ms','Ms.', 'Mr', 'Mr.',
'mr','MS','MS.','ms','ms.','Mis','miss','miss.','Mrs','Mrs.','mrs',
'mrs.','Madam','madam','ma"am']
While it’s possible to solve this task with conditional logic that uses if/else code blocks, such
an approach involves a lengthy code block. A simpler and easier solution involves the in keyword,
as shown here:
mr_dict={}
ms_dict={}
mrs_dict={}
titles = ['mr.','MR','MR.','mister','Mister','Ms','Ms.', 'Mr', 'Mr.',
'mr','MS','MS.','ms','ms.','Mis','miss','miss.','Mrs','Mrs.','mrs',
'mrs.','Madam','madam','ma"am']
for title in ['Mr','Mr.','mr','mr.','MR','MR.','mister','Mister']:
mr_dict[title] = "Mr"
for title in ['Ms','Ms.','MS','MS.','ms','ms.','Mis','miss','miss.']:
ms_dict[title] = "Ms"
for title in ['Mrs','Mrs.','mrs','mrs.','Madam','madam','ma"am']:
mrs_dict[title] = "Mrs"
print("Mr dictionary: ",mr_dict)
print()
print("Ms dictionary: ",ms_dict)
print()
print("Mrs dictionary:",mrs_dict)
Now launch the preceding code block and you will see the following output:
Mr dictionary: {'Mr': 'Mr', 'Mr.': 'Mr', 'mr': 'Mr', 'mr.': 'Mr', 'MR':
'Mr', 'MR.': 'Mr', 'mister': 'Mr', 'Mister': 'Mr'}
Ms dictionary: {'Ms': 'Ms', 'Ms.': 'Ms', 'MS': 'Ms', 'MS.': 'Ms', 'ms':
'Ms', 'ms.': 'Ms', 'Mis': 'Ms', 'miss': 'Ms', 'miss.': 'Ms'}
Mrs dictionary: {'Mrs': 'Mrs', 'Mrs.': 'Mrs', 'mrs': 'Mrs', 'mrs.':
'Mrs', 'Madam': 'Mrs', 'madam': 'Mrs', 'ma"am': 'Mrs'}
The previous solution makes it very easy to maintain, debug, and extend because the only
change that’s required is adding a new string in the appropriate location. Moreover, the given
solution does not require any additional loops or regular expressions. In the event that you need
a new category, such as “Sir,” define the dictionary and then add a new code
Python sir_dict
snippet, as shown here:
if title in ['Sir','sir','Sire','sire','Yessir','yessir']:
sir_dict[title] = "Sir"

Replace NULL With 0

Replace NULL Values With Average Value
This task involves two steps: first find the average of the non- values of a column in a
NULL
database table, and then update the values in that column with the value that you found in
NULL
the first step. Listing 3.1 displays the contents of the script
SQL replace_null_values.sql
that performs this pair of steps.
LISTING 3.1: replace_null_values.sql
USE mytools;
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS temperatures;
CREATE TABLE temperatures (temper INT, city CHAR(20));
INSERT INTO temperatures VALUES(78,'sf');
INSERT INTO temperatures VALUES(NULL,'sf');
INSERT INTO temperatures VALUES(42,NULL);
INSERT INTO temperatures VALUES(NULL,'ny');
SELECT * FROM temperatures;
SELECT @avg1 := AVG(temper) FROM temperatures;
update temperatures
set temper = @avg1
where ISNULL(temper);
SELECT * FROM temperatures;
Data Cleaning Tasks • 55
-- initialize city1 with the most frequent city value:
SELECT @city1 := (SELECT city FROM temperatures GROUP BY city ORDER
BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 1);
-- update NULL city values with the value of city1:
update temperatures
set city = @city1
where ISNULL(city);
SELECT * FROM temperatures;

An example of coalescing multiple values in an attribute involves replacing multiple strings

LISTING 3.2: reduce_values.sql
use mytools;
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS mytable;
CREATE TABLE mytable (str_date CHAR(15), state CHAR(20), reply
CHAR(10));
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20210915','New York','Yes');
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20211016','New York','no');
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20220117','Illinois','yes');
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20220218','New York','No');
SELECT * FROM mytable;
-- replace yes, Yes, y, Ys with Y:
update mytable
set reply = 'Y'
where upper(substr(reply,1,1)) = 'Y';
SELECT * FROM mytable;
-- replace all other values with N:
update mytable
set reply = 'N' where upper(substr(reply,1,1)) != 'Y';
SELECT * FROM mytable;

DROP TABLE IF EXISTS emp_details;

CONVERT STRINGS TO DATE VALUES

LISTING 3.4: str_to_date.sql
use mytools;
DROP TABLE IF EXISTS mytable;
CREATE TABLE mytable (str_date CHAR(15), state CHAR(20), reply
CHAR(10));
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20210915','New York','Yes');
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20211016','New York','no'););
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20220117','Illinois','yes'););
INSERT INTO mytable VALUES('20220218','New York','No'););
SELECT * FROM mytable;
-- 1) insert date-based feature:
ALTER TABLE mytable
Data Cleaning Tasks • 59
ADD COLUMN (real_date DATE);
SELECT * FROM mytable;
-- 2) populate real_date from str_date:
UPDATE mytable t1
INNER JOIN mytable t2
ON t1.str_date = t2.str_date
SET t1.real_date = DATE(t2.str_date);
SELECT * FROM mytable;
-- 3) Remove unwanted features:
ALTER TABLE mytable
DROP COLUMN str_date;
SELECT * FROM mytable;

ple in this section. However, after you read the code—it’s a one-liner—you will understand how
to adapt that code snippet to your own requirements (i.e., how to specify different delimiters).
Listing 3.5 displays the contents of and Listing 3.6 displays the contents of
delimiter1.txt
that replaces all delimiters with a comma (“,”).
delimiter1.sh
Data Cleaning Tasks • 61
LISTING 3.5: delimiter1.txt
1000|Jane:Edwards^Sales
2000|Tom:Smith^Development
3000|Dave:Del Ray^Marketing
LISTING 3.6: delimiter1.sh
cat delimiter1.txt | sed -e 's/:/,/' -e 's/|/,/' -e 's/\^/,/'
Listing 3.6 starts with the command line utility, which sends the contents of the file
cat
“standard output,” which is the screen (by default). However, in this example
delimiter1.txt
the output of this command becomes the input to the command because of the pipe (“|”)
sed
symbol.
The command consists of three parts, all of which are connected by the “-e” switch. You
sed
can think of “-e” as indicating “there is more processing to be done” by the command. In
sed
this example, there are three occurrences of “-e”, which means that the command will be
sed
invoked three times.
The first code snippet is , which translates into “replace each semicolon with a
's/:/,/'
comma.” The result of this operation is passed to the next code snippet, which is . This
's/|/,/'
code snippet translates into “replace each pipe symbol with a comma.” The result of this opera-
tion is passed to the next code snippet, which is \^ . This code snippet translates into
's/ /,/'
“replace each caret symbol (“^”) with a comma.” The result of this operation is sent to standard
output, which can be redirected to another text file. Launch the code in Listing 3.6 and you will
see the following output:
1000,Jane,Edwards,Sales
2000,Tom,Smith,Development
3000,Dave,Del Ray,Marketing
Three comments to keep in mind. First, the snippet contains a backslash because the caret
symbol (“^”) is a meta character, so we need to “escape” this character. The same is true for other
meta characters, such as “$” and “.”.
Second, you can easily extend the command for each new delimiter that you encounter
sed
as a field separator in a text file: simply follow the pattern that you see in Listing 3.6 for each
new delimiter.
Third, redirect the output of to the text file by launching
delimiter1.sh delimiter2.txt
the following command:
./delimiter1.sh > delimiter2.txt
If an error occurs in the preceding code snippet, make sure that is execut-
delimiter1.sh
able by invoking the following command:
chmod 755 delimiter1.sh

filename="variable_columns.csv"

BEGIN { colCount = 6 }
{

print ""

BEGIN

CSV
Data Cleaning Tasks • 63
LISTING 3.9: variable_columns2.sh
filename="variable_columns.csv"
cat $filename | awk -F"," '
BEGIN {
maxColCount = 0;
##############################################
# maxColCount = # of fields in the longest row
##############################################
while(getline line < ARGV[1]) {
colCount = split(line,data)
if(maxColCount < length(data)) {
maxColCount = length(data)
}
}
}
{
# print current input line:
printf("%s", $0)
# pad with NaN (if necessary):
for(j=NF; j<maxColCount; j++) {
printf(",NaN")
}
print ""
}
' $filename

awk

when this loop has completed, its value will be the maximum number of columns of the lines in

BEGIN {
colCount = 0; minColCount = 9999
###############################################
# minColCount = # of fields in the shortest row
###############################################

minColCount = length(data)
}
}
}

# perform for each input line:

}
print ""
}
' $filename

CONVERTING NUMERIC DATE FORMATS
This section shows you how to convert date formats to a common format of the form
. Before delving into the code, the following list contains sample formats for the
MM-DD-YYYY
month, day, and year of a date:
Data Cleaning Tasks • 69
•
yy: two-digit year (ex: 22)
•
yyyy: four-digit year (ex: 2022)
•
m: one-digit month (ex: 4)
•
mm: two-digit month (ex: 04)
•
mmm: three letters for month (ex: Apr)
•
mmmm: month spelled in full (ex: April)
•
d: one-digit day of the month (ex: 2)
•
dd: two-digit day of the month (ex: 02)
•
ddd: three letter day of week (ex: Sat)
•
dddd: day spelled in full (ex: Saturday)
Listing 3.16 displays the contents of that contains fictitious dates in various for-
dates.txt
mats, and the strings in bold have invalid formats.
LISTING 3.16: dates.txt
03/15/2021

dates.sh
ters from the dates in Listing 3.16 so that they have the same format.
LISTING 3.17: dates.sh
cat dates.txt | awk -F"/" '
{
DATE_FORMAT="valid"
# step 1: extract the month
if($0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}/) {
month = substr($0,1,2)
#print "normal month: ",month
} else if($0 ~ /^[0-9]\//) {
month = "0" substr($0,1,1)
#print "short month: ",month
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 2: extract the day
if( $0 ~ /^[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9][0-9]/ ) {
day = substr($0,4,2)
#print "normal day: ",day
} else if ( $0 ~ /^[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9]\// ) {
day = "0" substr($0,4,1)
#print "short day: ",day
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
70 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 3: extract the year
if( $0 ~ /^[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9][0-9][0-9][0-9]$/ ) {
year = substr($0,7,4)
#print "normal year: ",year
} else if ( $0 ~ /^[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9]\/[0-9][0-9]\/$/ ) {
year = "20" substr($0,7,2)
#print "short year: ",year
}
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
printf("=> $0: %s MM/DD/YYYY: %s-%s-%s\n",$0,month,day,year)
} else {
print "invalid format: ",$0
}
}
'
Listing 3.17 contains code that might seem daunting if you are unfamiliar with regular expres-
sions. Let’s try to demystify the code, starting with the RE that represents any single digit.
[0-9]
The initial caret symbol in indicates that a digit must appear in the leftmost (i.e., first)
^[0-9]\/
position. Next, the RE indicates that a “/” must follow the single digit. Thus, the string
^[0-9]\/
matches the RE but the strings , , and do not match the RE.
3/ 03/ B3/ AB/
Now let’s examine that RE represents any string that starts with two digits
^[0-9][0-9]\/
and then a “/” character. Thus, the string matches the RE but the strings , , and
03/ 3/ B3/ AB/
do not match the RE.
Next, the RE represents any string that starts with two digits,
^[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9][0-9]
followed by a “/” character, and then two more digits. Thus, the string matches the RE
03/15
but the strings , , and do not match the RE.
3/5 03/5 3/15
Finally, the RE matches a
^[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9][0-9]\/[0-9][0-9][0-9][0-9]$
string that:
• starts with two digits
• followed by a “/”
• another two digits
• followed by “/”
• four more digits
• no additional characters
As you can see, Listing 3.17 contains REs that match strings containing “short” months, days,
and years. Now launch the code in Listing 3.17 and you will see the following output:
=> $0: 03/15/2021 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-15-2021
=> $0: 3/15/2021 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-15-2021
=> $0: 3/15/21 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-15-2021
Data Cleaning Tasks • 71
=> $0: 03/5/2021 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-05-2021
=> $0: 3/5/2021 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-05-2021
=> $0: 3/5/21 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-05-2021
=> $0: 3/5/212 MM/DD/YYYY: 03-05-2021
=> $0: 3/5/21Z MM/DD/YYYY: 03-05-2021
Why are the preceding pair of lines (that are shown in bold) displayed as valid dates,
even though the anchor meta character “$” appears in the two REs for extracting the year?
Unfortunately, the latter two REs appear after the initial REs that extract the day value, and
those two REs do not check for invalid year formats.
Listing 3.18 displays the contents of that detects invalid dates, uses a shorthand
dates2.sh
notation for multiple digits, and removes extraneous print-related statements.
LISTING 3.18: dates2.sh
cat dates.txt | awk -F"/" '
{
DATE_FORMAT="valid"
# step 1: check for invalid formats
if($0 ~ /[A-Za-z]/) {
print "invalid characters: ",$0
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
} else if($0 ~ /\/[0-9]{3}$/) {
print "invalid format: ",$0
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 2: extract the month
if($0 ~ /^[0-9][0-9]/) {
month = substr($0,1,2)
} else if($0 ~ /^[0-9]\//) {
month = "0" substr($0,1,1)
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 3: extract the day
if( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\/[0-9]{2}/ ) {
day = substr($0,4,2)
} else if ( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\/[0-9]{2}\// ) {
day = "0" substr($0,4,1)
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 4: extract the year
if( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\/[0-9]{2}\/[0-9]{4}$/ ) {
72 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
year = substr($0,7,4)
} else if ( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\/[0-9]\/[0-9]{2}\/$/ ) {
year = "20" substr($0,7,2)
}
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
printf("$0: %10s => %s-%s-%s\n",$0,month,day,year)
} else {
print "Date format invalid:",$0
}
}
'
Listing 3.18 is very similar to contents of Listing 3.17 with some differences. The first differ-
ence is that Listing 3.18 checks for phone numbers that have alphabetic characters and reports
them as having an invalid format. The second difference is the conditional code block that identi-
fies any phone numbers that have three digits in the rightmost position.
The third difference is a shorthand way to specify multiple consecutive digits: [0-9]{2}
matches any pair of consecutive digits, [0-9]{3} matches any occurrence of three consecu-
tive digits, and so forth. Now launch the code in Listing 3.18 and you will see the following

. Listing 3.19 displays the contents of that contains dates in various

LISTING 3.20: dates3.sh
cat dates2.txt | tr -s ' ' |sed -e 's/- /-/g' -e 's/ /-/g' |awk -F"-" '
BEGIN {
months["JAN"] = "JAN"
months["FEB"] = "FEB"
months["MAR"] = "MAR"
months["APR"] = "APR"
months["MAY"] = "MAY"
months["JUN"] = "JUN"
months["JUL"] = "JUL"
months["AUG"] = "AUG"
months["SEP"] = "SEP"
months["OCT"] = "OCT"
months["NOV"] = "NOV"
months["DEC"] = "DEC"
}
{
#Valid date formats (Oracle):
#DD-MON-YY: 04-SEP-2022
#DD-MON-YYYY: 05-OCT-22
$0 = toupper($0)
DATE_FORMAT="valid"
# step 1: extract the day:
if($0 ~ /^[0-9]\-/) {
day = "0" substr($0,1,1)
} else if($0 ~ /^[0-9][0-9]\-/) {
day = substr($0,1,2)
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 2: extract the month:
if( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\-[A-Z]{3}/ ) {
month = substr($0,4,3)
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
}
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
# step 3: extract the year:
if( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\-[A-Z]{3}\-[0-9]{2}$/ ) {
year = "20" substr($0,8,2)
} else if( $0 ~ /^[0-9]{2}\-[A-Z]{3}\-[0-9]{4}/ ) {
year = substr($0,8,4)
} else {
DATE_FORMAT="invalid"
74 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
}
}
if(DATE_FORMAT="valid") {
if(months[month] == month) {
printf("%12s => %s-%s-%s\n",$0,month,day,year)
} else {
printf("Invalid month: %s-%s-%s\n",month,day,year)
}
} else {
print "Date format invalid:",$0
}
}
'

WORKING WITH DATE AND TIME DATE FORMATS
Now that you have seen code samples with dates of the form , this section han-
MM:DD:YYYY
dles dates of the form . Although the -based code sample in this
YYYY:MM:DD:HH:MM:SS awk
section is much longer than all other code samples in this book, it’s based on techniques that you
have already seen in previous date-related code samples. If you do not need to delve into the
details of this code sample at this time, feel free to skip this section with no loss of continuity.
Single digit values for all fields (except the year) are considered valid, and handling all the
possible combinations becomes quite tedious. However, the code sample in this section shows
you a shortcut that involves dynamically modifying the input line so that it’s progressively padded
as the input string is processed in a left-to-right fashion.
Data Cleaning Tasks • 75
To make sure this is clear, the string is a valid date, and as the code checks
3/15/22 5:5:44
for the validity of the individual date-related fields, the preceding string is successively modified
as follows:
3/15/22 5:5:44
03/15/22 5:5:44
03/15/2022 5:5:44
03/15/2022 5:5:44
03/15/2022 05:5:44
03/15/2022 05:05:44
As you can see, the final row in the preceding list of dates is a “fully padded” date. This
approach significantly reduces the number of patterns that need to be checked in order to deter-
mine whether or not a date has a valid format.
Listing 3.21 displays the contents of that contains dates, and Listing 3.22
dates-times.txt
shows you the contents of , which is an -based shell script that
date-times-padded.sh awk
processes the dates in Listing 3.21.
LISTING 3.21: date-times.txt
2/30/2020 15:05:44
2/30/2020 15:05:44
3/15/22 15:5:44
4/29/23 15:5:4
5/16/24 5:05:44
6/17/25 5:5:44
7/18/26 5:5:4
8/19/24 115:05:44
Notice that the first and last lines in Listing 3.21 are invalid: the first row contains a value of 30
for February, which is invalid for any year, and the last row contains an invalid value for the hour.

date-times-padded.sh
in Listing 3.21 have the format .

function check_leap_year(year) {
#########################################
# A year is a leap year if the following:
# 1) it is a multiple of 4 AND
# 2) a century must be a multiple of 400
# => 2000 is a leap year but 1900 is not.
#########################################
result = 0 # 0: non-leap year 1: leap year
if((year % 4) == 0) {
if((year % 100) == 0) {
if((year % 400) == 0) {
return 1 # leap year
} else {
76 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
return 0 # non-leap year
}
} else {
return 1 # leap year
}
} else {
return 0 # non-leap year
}
}

BEGIN { code = 10000; incr = 10000 }

}

{
printf("%d,%s\n", $1 + code, $2)
code += incr
}
' < $FILE

DATA CLEANING ON A KAGGLE DATASET

LISTING 3.29: convert_marketing.sh
# Kaggle dataset: https://www.kaggle.com/fehmifratpolat/marketing-report
# step 1: extract the 1st, 3rd, 4th, and 7th fields:
cat mark1.csv |awk -F";" '{print $1 ":" $3 ":" $4 ":" $7}' >mark2.csv
# step 2: replace Not tracked" with "direct":
# peri-Co-od;salesChannel;platformcode;marketingInvestment
# date;channel;platformcode;;spend
cat mark2.csv |awk -F":" '
{
$3 = tolower($3)
if( $3 ~ /Not tracked/ ) {
$3 = "direct"
} else if( $3 ~ /unpaid/ ) {
$3 = "organic"
} else if( $3 ~ /Silverpop/ ) {
$3 = "email"
}
if( $4 ~ /,/ ) {
split($4,arr1,",")
$4 = arr1[1] "." arr1[2]
}
print $1 ":" $2 ":" $3 ":" $4
}
' >mark3.csv
# step 3: replace YYYYDDMM with YYYY-MM-YY format:
cat mark3.csv |awk -F":" '
{
year = substr($1,1,4)
day = substr($1,5,2)
month = substr($1,7,2)
printf("%s-%s-%s:%s:%s:%s\n",year,month,day,$2,$3,$4)
}
Data Cleaning Tasks • 87
' >mark4.csv
# step 4: calculate subtotals based on $1+$2+$3:
cat mark4.csv |awk -F":" '
{
fields=$1FS$2FS$3;subtotals[fields] += $4
}
# step 5: display values of subtotals:
END{
total = 0
for (i in subtotals) {
printf("Subtotal for %44s => %-8d\n", i, subtotals[i])
total += subtotals[i]
}
printf("TOTAL REVENUE: => %44d\n",total)
}
' >mark5.csv
Although it’s not necessary to split the code into five awk scripts, it’s easier to follow the code,
and besides, you will also have access to the intermediate files that you can inspect to see their
contents.
Listing 3.29 starts with step 1 that extracts the 1st, 3rd, 4th, and 7th fields from the CSV file
mark1.csv and redirects the output to the CSV file mark2.csv, as shown here:
cat mark1.csv |awk -F";" '{print $1 ":" $3 ":" $4 ":" $7}' >mark2.csv
Step 2 in Listing 3.29 replaces occurrences of the string “Not tracked” with “direct”, the
string “unpaid” with “organic”, and the string “Silverpop” with email in $3. In addition, the “,”
that appears in $4 is replaced by a “.”, after which a new string is printed that contains the modi-
fications to $3 and $4. Each output line is redirected to the CSV file mark3.csv.
Step 3 in Listing 3.29 replaces dates with a format, as shown here,
YYYYDDMM YYYY-MM-YY
after which the output is redirected to the CSV file mark4.csv:
year = substr($1,1,4)
day = substr($1,5,2)
month = substr($1,7,2)
printf("%s-%s-%s:%s:%s:%s\n",year,month,day,$2,$3,$4)
Step 4 in Listing 3.29 is probably the most interesting code snippet in this code sample. The
concatenation of the first three fields, separated by the default field separator, form an index for
the array subtotals. Each time this combination appears in mark4.csv, the corresponding entry in
the subtotals array is incremented with the value in $4, as shown here:
fields=$1FS$2FS$3;subtotals[fields] += $4
After the body of this awk script has completed execution, an END block (which is labeled
step 5) displays the values in the array, along with the grand total, as shown here:
subtotals
total = 0
for (i in subtotals) {
printf("Subtotal for %44s => %-8d\n", i, subtotals[i])
total += subtotals[i]
}

Although the terminology regarding data wrangling is specific to this book and not necessarily
standard terminology, the distinction can be helpful when you are involved in discussions that
necessitate finer-grained terminology.

WHAT IS DATA WRANGLING?
Data wrangling means different things to different people. Some online articles suggest that
data wrangling involves a set of steps, and other articles suggest that data wrangling involves
converting datasets from one format to a different format. The following link describes data
wrangling as a six-step process:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_wrangling
In addition to the steps outlined in the preceding link, data wrangling can also involve the
following tasks (performed in code samples in Chapter 7):
92 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
• transforming datasets from one format into another format (convert)
• creating new datasets from subsets of columns in existing datasets (extract)
As you can see, the preceding steps differentiate between converting data to a different
format versus extracting data from multiple datasets to create new datasets. The conversion
.
process can be considered a data cleaning task if only the first step is performed; that is, there is
no extraction step.
In the previous chapter you learned about data cleaning, along with a collection of -
Python
based code samples and Unix shell scripts to perform data cleaning tasks. By contrast, data
wrangling involves multiple steps that can include transforming one or more files. The following
subsection provides a more detailed distinction between data cleaning and data transformation.
Data Transformation: What Does This Mean?
In general, data cleaning involves a single data source (not necessarily in a format), with
CSV
some type of modification to the content of the data source (e.g., filling missing values, changing
date formats, etc.), without creating a second data source.
For example, suppose that the data source is a table called that contains
MySQL employees
employee-related information. After data cleaning tasks on the table are completed,
employees
the result will still be named the table. In database terminology, data cleaning is
employees
somewhat analogous to executing a statement that involves a on a single table.
SQL SELECT
On the other hand, if two files contain different date formats and you need to create
CSV
a single file that is based on the date columns, then there will be some type of conversion
CSV
process that could be one of the following:
• convert the first date format to the second date format, or
• convert the second date format to the first date format, or
• convert both date formats to a third date format
In the case of financial data, you are likely to also encounter different currencies, which
involves a conversion rate between a pair of currencies. Since currency conversion rates fluctu-
ate, you need to decide the exchange rate to use for the data, which can be:
• the exchange rate during the date that the files were generated
CSV
• the current currency exchange rate
• some other mechanism
In addition, you might also need to convert the files to documents, where the latter
CSV XML
might be required to conform to an Schema, and perhaps also conform to , which is a
XML XBRL
requirement for business reporting purposes:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XBRL
As mentioned earlier, data transformation can involve two or more data sources in order
to create yet another data source whose attributes are in the required format. Here are four
scenarios of data transformation with just two data sources A and B, where data from A and
from B are combined in order to create data source C, where A, B, and C can have different
file formats:
Data Wrangling • 93
• all attributes in A and all attributes in B
• all attributes in A and a subset of attributes in B
• a subset of the attributes in A and all attributes in B
• a subset of the attributes in A and a subset of attributes in B
In database terminology, data transformation is somewhat analogous to executing a state-
SQL
ment that involves a on two or more database tables with a clause. Such state-
SELECT JOIN SQL
ments typically involve a subset of columns from each database table, which would correspond
to selecting a subset of the features in the data sources.
Keep in mind there is also the scenario involving the concatenation of two or more data
sources. If all data sources have the same attributes, then their concatenation is straightforward,
but you might also need to check for duplicate values. For example, if you want to load multiple
files into a database table that does not allow duplicates, then one solution involves concat-
CSV
enating the files from the command line and then excluding the duplicate rows.
CSV
The preceding concatenation task can be easily performed. Suppose that you have the fol-
lowing set of files (all of which have the same attributes) , , . . . ,
CSV emp1.csv emp2.csv
. Concatenate these files and exclude duplicate rows by creating a shell script called
emp100.csv
with the following contents:
concat.sh
for f in `ls emp*csv`
do
cat $f
done
Now make sure that the shell script is executable:
chmod +x concat.sh
Now launch the shell script that redirects its output to the command and
concat.sh sort
then redirects its output to the command, with the final output redirected to the file
uniq CSV
:
all_emps.csv
./concat.sh | sort | uniq > all_emps.csv
At this point you might be able to load the file into a table, pro-
CSV all_emps.csv MySQL
vided that that table is not a “child” table of another table, with a condition on the
CONSTRAINT
rows of the child table. For example, a table typically has a one-to-many relationship
customers
with a table, which means that you cannot insert rows in the latter table if
purchase_orders
they do not have an associated customer row in the table.
customers
When in doubt, try loading the file into an appropriate table: if you encounter any errors,
CSV
make the necessary corrections until the loading process is successful.
This concludes the discussion regarding the first section for data wrangling, and the next
several sections contain additional data cleaning code samples, after which you will see data
wrangling code samples.

# Records are separated by blank lines.
input_file="multi_line_records.csv"
awk '
BEGIN { RS = "" ; FS = "\n" }
{
printf("%s,%s,%s\n", $1, $2, $3)
}
' < $input_file

LISTING 4.8: split_quotes.sh
# Records are separated by blank lines
# PART 1: create a single string
interfile1="temp_single_line1.csv"
interfile2="temp_single_line2.csv"
interfile3="temp_single_line3.csv"
csv_file="multi_line3.csv"
cat $csv_file | awk -F"," '
BEGIN { count = 1; colCount = 3 }
{
printf("%s", $0)
if( $0 !~ /,$/ ) {
printf(",")
} else {
if( $0 !~ /^[0-9]/ ) {
if( count % colCount == 0) {
printf(",")
}
count += 1
}
}
}' > $interfile1
# id,fname,lname,1,donna,rhymes?,2,dave,"jones$",3,john,van jones.#,4,
stevie,"ray,vaughn#",5,ralph,emerson#,
# PART 2: replace patterns like "abc,def" with "abcZdef"
cat $interfile1 | awk -F"," '
{
for(i=1; i<=NF; i++) {
if( $i ~ /^"/ && $(i+1) ~ /"$/ ) {
printf("%sZ",$i)
} else {
printf("%s,",$i)
}
}
Data Wrangling • 99
}
' > $interfile2
# PART 3: ensure that each line contains three fields
cat $interfile2 | awk -F',' '
BEGIN { start_quote = 0; end_quote = 0; quote = 0; colCount = 3 }
{
for(i=1; i<=NF; i++) {
if($i ~ /^"/ && $i ~ /"$/ ) {
printf("%s",$i)
}
else if($i !~ /^"/ && $i !~ /"$/ ) {
printf("%s,", $i)
}
else {
if($i ~ /^"/ ) {
printf("%s,",$i)
quote = 1
start_quote = 1
} else {
if($i ~ /"$/ ) {
printf("%s",$i)
quote = 1
end_quote = 1
}
}
}
if( quote == 0) {
if(i % colCount == 0) {
print ""
}
} else {
if(start_quote == 1 && end_quote == 1) {
quote = 0
start_quote = 0
end_quote = 0
}
}
}
}
' > $interfile3
cat $interfile3 | sed -e 's/Z/,/' -e '/^,,/d' -e 's/,$//'

As you can see, the preceding code snippet checks if field starts with a quote (“) and also

if field ends with a quote (”), in which case the letter is printed.

put lines that contain three fields. Fields that contain no quotes or fields that contain balanced

OVERVIEW OF THE EVENTS PROJECT
As you saw in the introduction for this chapter, this section contains a project to generate a
file that contains the details of the concerts (including the dates and locations) pertaining to
CSV
various music bands that are scheduled to perform in different countries.
Although this project might appear to be straightforward, in actuality there are some tedious
details, such as which programming language (Unix shell programming, , , or per-
Python Java
haps other languages) to adopt for transforming each data source into a file.
CSV
Data Wrangling • 101
Why This Project?
Before delving into the details, you might be pondering this legitimate question: why create
a project that involves significant complexity? The intent is to simulate a scenario in which you
will be required to work with text files that are in different formats, which might be file formats
that are unfamiliar to you, and perhaps even file formats that you wish could be avoided (yes, it
does happen).
From another perspective, this project is intended to take you out of your comfort zone, for
the purpose of enhancing your technical skills. As such, the nature of the project is of secondary
importance: if you prefer, you could easily replace music bands with soccer clubs from countries
throughout the world, or chess tournaments involving chess players from various countries (you
get the idea).
Returning to the project: the transformation from the source files to files is performed
CSV
via a mixture of shell scripts, scripts, and code. As you learn how to convert each
Python Java
source file into a -based file using different technologies, you can assess their suitability for
CSV

Python
have approximately the same length, but you need to take into account the readability and main-
tainability of the code.
By way of comparison, the code for converting an document to a file is
Python XML CSV
much shorter than the corresponding code, which is one of several factors involved in
Java
deciding which technology to use for converting the various files to a common format.
CSV
Indeed, there are multiple solutions that are possible for this project, and the “best” imple-
mentation is the one that works best for your requirements. Perhaps the central idea of this
project is to illustrate a key aspect of data wrangling: how to transform data sources in order to
create a new data source that is derived from the initial set of data sources.

sed

GENERATING CITY CODES FROM COUNTRY CODES: AWK
Listing 4.11 displays the contents of that illustrates how to
construct_city_codes.sh
generate a unique code for every city that belongs to a given country.
LISTING 4.11: construct_city_codes.sh
FILE1="country_codes.csv"
FILE2="countries_cities.csv"
rm -f city_codes.csv
awk -F"," '
BEGIN {
FS=","; OUT_FILE="city_codes.csv";
prev_name = ""; city_name = "";
start_code=100; curr_code = 100;
city_code = ""; incr_code = 100
printf("=> BEGIN-PROCESSING: %s\n",ARGV[1])
while(getline line < ARGV[1]){
#format: country-code,country-name
split(line,data)
code = data[1]
country = data[2]
#printf("code: %s country: %s\n",code,country)
country_code[country] = code
code_country[code] = country
}
close(ARGV[1])
}

import csv
import org.w3c.dom.Document;
import org.w3c.dom.Element;
import org.w3c.dom.Node;
import org.w3c.dom.NodeList;
import org.xml.sax.SAXException;
import javax.xml.parsers.DocumentBuilder;
import javax.xml.parsers.DocumentBuilderFactory;
import javax.xml.parsers.ParserConfigurationException;
import java.io.File;
import java.io.FileWriter;
import java.io.IOException;
import java.io.InputStream;
public class BandsToCSV
{
private static final String FILENAME = "bands.xml";
public static void main(String[] args) {
String filename = "bandmembers.csv";
File outfile = null;
FileWriter csvWriter = null;
// 1) create a file object:
outfile = new File(filename);
// 2) create a FileWriter object:
try {
Data Wrangling • 111
csvWriter = new FileWriter("bandmembers.csv");
} catch (IOException e) {
System.out.println("An error occurred:");
e.printStackTrace();
}
// 3) process the XML file:
DocumentBuilderFactory dbf =
DocumentBuilderFactory.newInstance();
try {
DocumentBuilder db = dbf.newDocumentBuilder();
Document doc = db.parse(new File(FILENAME));
doc.getDocumentElement().normalize();
System.out.println("ROOT: " +
doc.getDocumentElement().getNodeName());
NodeList bandList = doc.getElementsByTagName("band");
for (int idx = 0; idx < bandList.getLength(); idx++) {
Node node = bandList.item(idx);
if (node.getNodeType() == Node.ELEMENT_NODE) {
Element element = (Element) node;
// 4) the id of the band:
String band_id = element.getElementsByTagName("band_id")
.item(0).getTextContent();
band_id = band_id.replaceAll("\\s", "");
band_id = band_id.replaceAll("\\n", "");
System.out.println("band_id = "+band_id);
// 5) the name of the band:
String band_name =
element.getElementsByTagName("band_name")
.item(0).getTextContent();
band_name = band_name.trim();
band_name = band_name.replaceAll("\\n", "");
System.out.println("band_name = "+band_name);
// 6) the band members:
NodeList memberList =
element.getElementsByTagName("band_member");
for (int idx2=0; idx2<memberList.getLength(); idx2++)
{
Node memberNode = memberList.item(idx2);
if (memberNode.getNodeType() == Node.ELEMENT_NODE)
{
Element element2 = (Element) memberNode;
System.out.println("Current Element: " +
memberNode.getNodeName());
String band_member =
112 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
memberList.item(idx2).getTextContent();
//band_member = band_member.replaceAll("\\s", "");
band_member = band_member.trim();
band_member = band_member.replaceAll("\\n", "");
System.out.println("band_member = "+band_member);
// 7) write a string to the CSV file:
csvWriter.write(band_id+","+
band_name+","+
band_member+"\n");
}
}
}
}
csvWriter.close();
} catch (ParserConfigurationException | SAXException | IOException e)
{
e.printStackTrace();
}
}
}

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
# 1) read the input file concert_dates.csv
# header line: band_code,country_code,city,date,price
# sample row: 100,039,vicenza,01/05/2022,50
concert_dates_file="concert_dates.csv"
# 2) read python_bandmembers.csv (band names and members)
# sample row:100_The Data Wranglers_Dave Pallai
band_members_file="python_bandmembers.csv"
# 3) read country_codes.csv (country and code)
# sample row: 039,italy
cc_codes_file="country_codes.csv"
# 4) create the output file calendar_coe.csv:
# header line: band_code,band_name,country_code,country_name,
city,date,price
# sample row: The Data Wranglers,italy,vicenza,01/05/2022,50
outfile1="calendar_coe.csv"
# 5) populate three data frames:
concert1_df = pd.read_csv(concert_dates_file)
band2_df = pd.read_csv(band_members_file)
cc3_df = pd.read_csv(cc_codes_file)
# 6) add column labels:
#existing row: 100,039,vicenza,01/05/2022,50
concert1_df.columns = ['band_code','country_code','City','Concert
Date','Tickets (USD)']
band2_df.columns = ['band_code','band_name','band_members']
cc3_df.columns = ['country_code','country_name']
Data Wrangling • 117
# 7) extract array of unique band codes and band names:
bands_unique_codes = band2_df.band_code.unique()
bands_unique_names = band2_df.band_name.unique()
# 8) create dictionary from unique band codes and band names:
bands_codes_names_dict = {}
for code, name in zip(bands_unique_codes,bands_unique_names):
bands_codes_names_dict[code] = name
# 9) insert a column for "Band Name":
concert1_df['Band Name'] = concert1_df['band_code'].map(bands_codes_
names_dict)
# 10) create dictionary from unique country codes and names:
country_code_to_name_dict = {}
for index, row in cc3_df.iterrows():
country_code = row['country_code']
country_name = row['country_name']
country_code_to_name_dict[country_code] = country_name
# 11) insert a column for country names:
concert1_df['Country'] = concert1_df['country_code'].map(country_code_
to_name_dict)
# 12) remove band codes and country codes:
del concert1_df['band_code']
del concert1_df['country_code']
print("*** Concert Details ***")
print(concert1_df)
print()
# 13) save concert details to CSV file:
concert1_df.to_csv(outfile1, index=False)

eature eleCtion
This chapter explores various aspects of feature selection in machine learning, such as
available feature selection techniques and how to decide which feature selection tech-
nique to use for a given dataset. You will also learn about algorithms that require scaled
data, how to work with imbalanced datasets, and how to apply transformations to data in
datasets.
The first part discusses techniques for feature selection, which involves selecting a subset
of the features in a given dataset. Note that feature extraction is discussed in the next chapter.
The second part discusses techniques for processing imbalanced datasets, such as oversam-
pling, undersampling, and a combination of both techniques. This section also describes
SMOTE
for generating synthetic data in tabular datasets.
WHAT IS FEATURE SELECTION?
Feature selection is crucial for building efficient and effective machine learning models. It’s
important to note that the best feature selection method may vary depending on the nature of
the data and the problem you’re trying to solve.
Feature selection is an essential process in machine learning wherein you select those features
in your data that contribute most to the prediction variable or output. The goal is to improve the
model’s performance, reduce overfitting, and reduce the computational cost. Feature selection
is important for several reasons, some of which are listed as follows:
• reduces overfitting by removing redundant or irrelevant features
• improves accuracy by considering only relevant features
• reduces training time due to fewer data dimensions
Three Types of Feature Selection Methods
There are several categories of feature selection methods (each containing multiple
methods) that you can use in machine learning, some of which are listed as follows, followed by
subsections that provide additional information:
124 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
• filter methods
• wrapper methods
• embedded methods
FILTER METHODS
Filter methods select features based on general metrics like correlation. Filter methods for
feature selection evaluate the relevance of the features based on their inherent characteristics
and statistical properties. Moreover, they are independent of any machine learning algorithms.
In a sense, filter methods are foundational and are often the first step in the feature selection
process. They are quick to compute and provide a good understanding of the data’s characteris-
tics, but they consider each feature individually, rather than in combination with others, which
might lead to suboptimal feature sets in some cases. The following subsections discuss some
popular filter methods for feature selection, along with Python code snippets.
Variance Threshold
This method removes features whose variance is below a certain threshold (hence its name),
which is based on the assumption that low variance features contain less information. Here is a
sample code block that involves variance threshold:
from sklearn.feature_selection import VarianceThreshold
# Sample data
X = [[0, 1, 0.5], [0, 1, 0.7], [0, 1, 0.8], [0, 1, 0.9]]
# Create VarianceThreshold object
selector = VarianceThreshold(threshold=0.01)
X_new = selector.fit_transform(X)
Chi-Squared Test
This test measures the dependence between stochastic variables. As such, this test is useful
for categorical features to evaluate the likelihood that any observed difference between the cat-
egories arises by chance. Here is a sample code block that involves the chi-squared test:
from sklearn.datasets import load_iris
from sklearn.feature_selection import SelectKBest, chi2
X, y = load_iris(return_X_y=True)
X_new = SelectKBest(chi2, k=2).fit_transform(X, y)
ANOVA F-test
This method is used for numerical input and categorical output. It estimates the degree of linear
dependency between two random variables. Here is a sample code block that involves an F-test:
from sklearn.datasets import load_iris
from sklearn.feature_selection import SelectKBest, f_classif
Feature Selection • 125
X, y = load_iris(return_X_y=True)
X_new = SelectKBest(f_classif, k=2).fit_transform(X, y)
Mutual Information
This technique measures the dependency between two variables, and its value is zero if and
only if two random variables are independent. Here is a code block that uses mutual information:
from sklearn.datasets import load_iris
from sklearn.feature_selection import SelectKBest, mutual_info_classif
X, y = load_iris(return_X_y=True)
X_new = SelectKBest(mutual_info_classif, k=2).fit_transform(X, y)
Correlation Coefficient
Features that are highly correlated with the target can be preferred, whereas features that
are correlated with each other instead of the target are candidates for removal. Here is a code
block that removes a correlated feature:
import pandas as pd
# Sample data
df = pd.DataFrame({
'A': [1, 2, 3, 4],
'B': [1.1, 2.1, 3.1, 4.1],
'C': [5, 6, 7, 8],
'target': [0, 1, 0, 1]
})
# Calculate correlations
correlations = df.corr()['target'].abs().sort_values(ascending=False)
selected_features = correlations.index[1:] # Excluding the target
itself
Another major category of feature selection techniques involves wrapper methods, which is
the topic of the next section.
WRAPPER METHODS
Wrapper methods use a model to evaluate the importance of features based on the per-
formance of a specific machine learning algorithm. These methods search for the best subset
of features by training the model on various feature subsets and selecting the best subset of
features.
Keep in mind that some of these methods, such as (discussed later in this chap-
Boruta
ter) and the from , require additional libraries to be
SequentialFeatureSelector mlxtend
installed. Moreover, wrapper methods can be computationally expensive, especially for high-
dimensional datasets, as they involve training a model multiple times.
The following subsections discuss some popular wrapper methods for feature selection,
along with code blocks that you can use to create complete code samples for your use
Python
cases.
126 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE)
RFE is a method that uses recursion in order to rank features based on the given estimator’s
importance. A code block that uses RFE is shown as follows:
from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
from sklearn.feature_selection import RFE
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
X,y = make_classification(n_samples=1000,n_features=100,n_informative=2)
estimator = LogisticRegression(max_iter=500)
selector = RFE(estimator, n_features_to_select=5, step=1)
X_new = selector.fit_transform(X, y)
Recursive Feature Elimination With Cross-Validation (RFECV)
RFECV extends RFE by using cross-validation to select the best number of features, as
shown in the following code block:
from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
from sklearn.feature_selection import RFECV
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=100,
n_informative=5)
estimator = LogisticRegression(max_iter=500)
selector = RFECV(estimator, step=1, cv=5)
X_new = selector.fit_transform(X, y)
Sequential Feature Selection (SFS)
SFS is a family of algorithms that adds or removes features based on a criterion, as shown in
the following code block:
from mlxtend.feature_selection import SequentialFeatureSelector
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=25,
n_informative=3)
knn = KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=4)
sfs = SequentialFeatureSelector(knn, k_features=5, forward=True,
floating=False)
X_new = sfs.fit_transform(X, y)
Backward Feature Elimination
This technique works as follows: start with the full set of features, and then remove one fea-
ture at a time until the desired number features (or performance criterion) is reached. A sample
code block is shown as follows:
from mlxtend.feature_selection import SequentialFeatureSelector
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
Feature Selection • 127
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=25,
n_informative=3)
knn = KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=4)
sbs = SequentialFeatureSelector(knn, k_features=5, forward=False,
floating=False)
X_new = sbs.fit_transform(X, y)
Boruta
Boruta is a feature selection algorithm that identifies all relevant features by comparing the
importance of the original set of attributes with importance achievable at random. A sample
code block is shown as follows:
from boruta import BorutaPy
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=25,
n_informative=3)
rf = RandomForestClassifier(n_jobs=-1, class_weight='balanced',
max_depth=5)
boruta_selector = BorutaPy(rf, n_estimators='auto', verbose=0,
random_state=1)
X_new = boruta_selector.fit_transform(X, y)
A third major category of feature selection techniques involves embedded methods, which is
the topic of the next section.
EMBEDDED METHODS
Embedded methods are algorithms that have built-in feature selection methods. In other
words, embedded methods are feature selection techniques that are integrated into the training
process of a machine learning algorithm. They exploit the model’s learning process to evaluate
the importance of each feature, and some of them inherently perform feature selection as a part
of the training.
Embedded methods are efficient because they perform feature selection and model train-
ing simultaneously. The selected features are specifically tailored for the chosen model, which
can result in better performance compared to filter and wrapper methods. Here’s a list of some
popular embedded methods for feature selection, followed by code samples:
Python
• L1 regularization (Lasso)
• decision trees (and tree-based models)
• elastic net
• LightGBM
• recursive feature elimination (RFE)
L1 Regularization (Lasso)
L1 regularization adds a penalty to the absolute values of coefficients. Features with zero
coefficients after the training are considered unimportant.
128 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
from sklearn.linear_model import Lasso
from sklearn.datasets import make_regression
X, y = make_regression(n_samples=1000, n_features=100, n_informative=10)
lasso = Lasso(alpha=0.1)
lasso.fit(X, y)
selected_features = [i for i, j in enumerate(lasso.coef_) if j != 0]
Decision Trees (and Tree-Based Models)
Tree-based algorithms like decision trees, random forests, and gradient boosted trees rank
features based on their importance derived from the number of splits for a given tree node as
well as the depth of the tree. Here is a code block that uses a random forest classifier:
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=25, n_
informative=5)
clf = RandomForestClassifier()
clf.fit(X, y)
importances = clf.feature_importances_
selected_features = [i for i, j in enumerate(importances) if j > 0.05]
Elastic Net
Elastic net combines L1 and L2 regularization, and it can be useful if there are multiple cor-
related features. Here is a code block that uses elastic net:
from sklearn.linear_model import ElasticNet
from sklearn.datasets import make_regression
X, y = make_regression(n_samples=1000, n_features=100, n_informative=10)
en = ElasticNet(alpha=0.1, l1_ratio=0.5)
en.fit(X, y)
selected_features = [i for i, j in enumerate(en.coef_) if j != 0]
LightGBM
LightGBM is a gradient boosting framework that uses tree-based learning algorithms.
It offers built-in feature importance evaluations. Here is a code block that uses LightGBM:
from lightgbm import LGBMClassifier
from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=25,
n_informative=5)
lgbm = LGBMClassifier()
lgbm.fit(X, y)
importances = lgbm.feature_importances_
selected_features = [i for i, j in enumerate(importances) if j > 50]
Feature Selection • 129
NOTE LightGBM requires an additional library to be installed.
Linear Models with Recursive Feature Elimination
RFE can be combined with any estimator that exposes the or
coef_ feature_impor-
attribute after fitting a model to the data. Here is a code block that uses RFE:
tances_
from sklearn.feature_selection import RFE
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.datasets import make_classification
X, y = make_classification(n_samples=1000, n_features=100,
n_informative=5)
estimator = LogisticRegression(max_iter=500)
selector = RFE(estimator, n_features_to_select=5, step=1)
X_new = selector.fit_transform(X, y)
THE NEED FOR FEATURE SCALING AND TRANSFORMATIONS
There are several reasons for scaling the data values in a dataset, two of which are listed here:
• some machine learning algorithms are affected by unscaled data
• some machine learning algorithms assume data is normally distributed
In particular, machine learning algorithms that apply distance-based metrics to dataset fea-
tures are affected by unscaled data, which includes the following algorithms:
• kNN (k nearest neighbor, which is a classification algorithm)
• kMeans (a clustering algorithm)
You can apply the following techniques to ensure that features are scaled:
• StandardScaler
• MinMaxScaler
• RobustScaler
In addition to scaling features, some machine learning algorithms assume that data is nor-
mally distributed. In particular, the following algorithms make this assumption:
• linear regression (a regression algorithm)
• logistic regression (a classification algorithm)
If you want to transform the data in a dataset so that it has a different distribution, the follow-
ing transformations will transform features into normally distributed features:
• exponential transformation
• logarithmic transformation
130 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
• reciprocal transformation
• square root transformation
The preceding list of scaling algorithms and transformation algorithms are discussed in the
NOTE
next chapter.
LABELED, UNLABELED, AND MULTICLASS CLASSIFICATION
Classification algorithms and regression algorithms work with labeled data, which means that
there is a so-called target column in the dataset whose values identify the associated row. If the
target column contains nonnumerical values, you can use the map() function to replace
Pandas
those values with numeric values. For example, if a gender feature contains the values male and
female, replace with numeric values as shown here ( is a pre-populated ):
df PandasDataFrame
df['gender'] = df['gender'].map({'male':0, 'female':1})
The following subsection contains some additional details regarding labeled and unlabeled
data.
Labeled Versus Unlabeled Data
Labeled data is often preferred because it saves you from performing the task of labeling the
data yourself. However, most data are unlabeled, and the cost of labeling that data can be pro-
hibitive. While it’s straightforward and inexpensive (e.g., Mechanical Turk) to manually label pic-
tures of dogs and cats, it’s obviously much more costly to label x-rays that contain signs of tumors.
One technique involves the use of a clustering algorithm (you can try several such algorithms)
to create an optimal set of clusters, after which you can assign the same label to all the items in
each cluster. However, there is an important consideration if you use this approach: is the accu-
racy of the labels good enough for your use case? For instance, if a clustering algorithm provides
80% accuracy and you need 90% accuracy, then you need explore how to improve the accuracy
of labeled data if you proceed with the newly labeled dataset.
WORKING WITH IMBALANCED DATASETS
Imbalanced datasets contain at least one class that has significantly more values than another
class in the dataset. For example, if class A has 99% of the data and class B has 1%, which clas-
sification algorithm would you use? Unfortunately, classification algorithms don’t work as well
with this type of imbalanced dataset.
Imbalanced classification involves processing imbalanced datasets, and the following list
contains several well-known techniques:
• random resampling rebalances the class distribution
• random undersampling deletes examples from the majority class
• random oversampling duplicates data in the minority class
• SMOTE (synthetic minority oversampling technique)
Feature Selection • 131
Random resampling rebalances the class distribution by resampling the data space in order to
reduce the discrepancy between the number of rows in the majority class and the minority class.
On the other hand, the random undersampling technique removes samples that belong to
the majority class from the dataset, and involves the following:
• randomly removes samples from majority class
• can be performed with or without replacement
• alleviates imbalance in the dataset
• may increase the variance of the classifier
• may discard useful or important samples
However, random undersampling does not work so well with extremely unbalanced datasets,
such as a 99% and 1% split into two classes. Moreover, undersampling can result in losing infor-
mation that is useful for a model.
Random oversampling generates new samples from a minority class: this technique dupli-
cates examples from the minority class.
Another option to consider is the package in the
Python imbalanced-learn scikit-
project. This project provides various resampling techniques for datasets that
learn-contrib
exhibit class imbalance. More details are here:
https://github.com/scikit-learn-contrib/imbalanced-learn
Another well-known technique is called , which involves data augmentation (i.e., syn-
SMOTE
thesizing new data samples). was initially developed by means of the algorithm (other
SMOTE kNN
options are available), and it can be an effective technique for handling imbalanced classes.
is discussed in more detail in a later section.
SMOTE
Detecting Imbalanced Data
This straightforward step involves counting the number of rows that are associated with each
class. First read the dataset into a data frame (let’s call it ) and then invoke the fol-
Pandas df
lowing code snippet:
df['your-target-column'].value_counts()
An example of the output from the preceding code snippet is here:
[OUT]
1 21000
0 6000
As you can see in the previous output, class 1 is more than three times larger than class 0,
so this column in the dataset is imbalanced.
The -based open-source library has a vast set of algorithms for
Python Scikit-Learn
machine learning, some of which support a parameter, as listed here:
class_weight
132 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
• LogisticRegression
• Perceptron
• RandomForest
• SVM
Simply set the value of the parameter equal to before training
class_weight balanced
the chosen model. Incidentally, it might also be worthwhile to train the chosen model with the
default value for the parameter. Check the online Scikit-Learn documentation
class_weight
for more information.
Rebalancing Datasets
Let’s return to the example of a dataset for which class A has 99% of the data and class B
has 1%. Which classification algorithm would you use? The following list contains several well-
known techniques for handling imbalanced datasets (not in any particular order):
• random resampling (rebalances the class distribution)
• random oversampling (duplicates data in the minority class)
• random undersampling (deletes examples from the majority class)
• algorithm selection
• cross-validation for imbalanced data
• generating synthetic data (e.g., SMOTE)
• performance metric selection
Several of the techniques in the preceding list are discussed in the following subsections.
Specify Stratify in Data Splits
This step also is straightforward because Scikit-Learn supports a parameter that
stratify
ensures the data is split so that the train data and test data contain the same proportion of class
values. For example, if a dataset contains 60% and 40%, respectively, of class and class in
A B
a column, then the train data and test data will contain the same proportions for class and .
A B
The following code block demonstrates how to split a dataset where and have already
X y
been initialized so that the data is stratified:
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
X_train,X_test,y_train,y_test = train_test_split(X,
y,
test_size=0.25,
random_state=42,
stratify=y)
FEATURE IMPORTANCE
You probably want to know which features in a dataset are significant as well as their relative
importance. For example, the number of bedrooms and bathrooms in a house are significant
features that affect the price of a house, whereas the ticket number for an airplane ticket is most
likely not a significant feature.
Feature Selection • 133
Suppose that a dataset has a “target” column, which is a column whose values we want
to predict during the testing phase (i.e., after the training phase). A target column exists
in datasets for classification tasks (which includes linear regression and classification algo-
rithms), whereas datasets without a target column are clustering tasks. In essence, the pro-
cess of assigning labels to an unlabeled dataset results in the creation a target column in a
dataset.
The key idea pertaining to feature importance involves assigning a numeric score between 0
and 100 that is assigned to each feature: this numeric score is a measure of the extent to which
that feature can predict the target variable. The conditions for assigning feature importance are
summarized here:
• select all the relevant features
• assign a value between 0 and 100 to each feature
• ensure the sum of the feature values is 100
As a side note: if we divide the feature values by 100 we have a probability distribution.
One straightforward way to determine feature importance involves the random forest algo-
rithm in . For a given feature, calculates its importance (which is a numeric
Sklearn Sklearn
value) by examining the average reduction in impurity in the set of tree nodes that involve that
feature. This calculation is performed for each feature after the training step has completed,
after which the values are scaled (essentially a probability distribution) to simplify the compari-
son of the importance of features.
Listing 5.1 displays the contents of that shows you how to deter-
feature_importance.py
mine the important features in the dataset as well as their relative importance.
Titanic
LISTING 5.1: feature_importance.py
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
df = pd.read_csv("titanic2.csv",
usecols = ['survived','sex','age','class','embarked'])
y = df.pop("survived")
# convert categories to numeric values:
df['sex'] = df['sex'].replace({'male':1, "female":0})
df['class'] = df['class'].replace({'First':1, "Second":2, "Third":3})
df['embarked'] = df['embarked'].replace({'S':0, "C":1, "Q":2})
# perform train test split:
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(df, y,
test_size=0.2, random_state=42)
rng = np.random.RandomState(0)
random_forest = RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=10, random_state=rng)
random_forest.fit(X_train,y_train)
134 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering

feature_imp = pd.DataFrame({'feature':X_train.columns,'importance':
np.round(random_forest.feature_importances_,3)})
feature_imp = feature_imp.sort_values('importance',ascending=False).
set_index('feature')
print("feature_imp.head():")
print(feature_imp.head())

WHAT IS SMOTE?
(synthetic minority oversampling technique) is a technique for synthesizing new
SMOTE
samples for a dataset, which is data augmentation (i.e., synthesizing new data samples) that is
performed before you use a classification algorithm. was initially developed by means of
SMOTE
the algorithm (other options are available), and it can be an effective technique for handling
kNN
imbalanced classes. This technique is based on linear interpolation:
• Step 1: select samples that are close in the feature space
• Step 2: draw a line between the samples in the feature space
• Step 3: draw a new sample at a point along that line
Feature Selection • 135
A more detailed explanation of the SMOTE algorithm is here:
• select a random sample “a” from the minority class
• now find k nearest neighbors for that example
• select a random neighbor “b” from the nearest neighbors
• create a line L that connects “a” and “b”
• randomly select one or more points “c” on line L
If need be, you can repeat this process for the other (k-1) nearest neighbors in order to dis-
tribute the synthetic values more evenly among the nearest neighbors.
SMOTE Extensions
The initial algorithm is based on the classification algorithm, which has been
SMOTE kNN
extended in various ways, such as replacing with . A list of extensions is shown
kNN SVM SMOTE
as follows:
• borderline-SMOTE (kNN)
• borderline-SMOTE (SVM)
• adaptive synthetic sampling (ADASYN)
• DBSMOTE
Perform an Internet search for more details about these algorithms, and also navigate to the
following URL:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oversampling_and_undersampling_in_data_analysis
An Alternative to SMOTE
The following article presents an alternative to SMOTE and undersampling:
https://medium.com/@ali.soleymani.co/stop-using-smote-to-treat-class-imbalance-take-this-
intuitive-approach-instead-9cb822b8dc45
According to the preceding article, SMOTE and undersampling do not achieve high levels
of recall and also additional complexity. The author provides another technique in the preceding
link, which is applied to a dataset that contains two classes, with the following values:
Nmin = number of elements in the minority class
Nmaj = number of elements in the majority class
Now find a pair of positive numbers that satisfy the following:
Nmin * Wmin = Nmaj * Wmaj
Now assign the values Wmin and Wmaj as the weights of the elements in the minority class
and majority class, respectively. One simple solution involves calculating the LCM (lowest com-
mon multiple) of Nmin and Nmaj, and then computing values for Wmin and Wmax as follows:
136 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
lcm = lowest common multiple of Nmin and Nmaj = LCM(Nmin,Nmaj)
Wmin = lcm/Nmin
Wmax = lcm/Nmax
Example #1:
Nmin = 500
Nmaj = 1200
Since Nmaj:Nmin = 12:5, select values for Wmin and Wmaj such that Wmin:Wmaj = 12:5.
In addition, , which means that:
LCM(Nmin,Nmaj) = 6000
Wmin = lcm/Nmin = 6000/500 = 12
Wmax = lcm/Nmax = 6000/1200 = 5
Example #2:
Nmin = 50
Nmaj = 950
Since Nmaj:Nmin = 19:1, select values for Wmin and Wmaj such that Wmin:Wmaj = 19:1.
In addition, , which means that:
LCM(Nmin,Nmaj) = 950
Wmin = lcm/Nmin = 950/50 = 19
Wmax = lcm/Nmax = 950/950 = 1
The link at the beginning of this section describes the preceding technique in greater detail,
along with the results of training models using this technique (including comparison charts).
WHAT ARE TRANSFORMS?
Transforms involve applying functions to a dataset in order to achieve linearity, after which
you might gain insights into the transformed data that were not apparent in the original datasets.
For instance, if the transformed data resembles a linear-like distribution, use linear regression to
find the best-fitting line for the transformed data.
You can apply a transformation to one or to both of the variables. For example, a logarithmic
transformation calculates the logarithm of the independent variable and makes no modification
to the dependent variable. On the other hand, a power transformation calculates the logarithm
of the independent variable as well as the dependent variable.
Keep in mind that machine learning algorithms that use the Euclidean distance between
pairs of values are biased toward the larger class in imbalanced datasets. Therefore, it’s impor-
tant that you scale the values in the dataset, which can be performed via standardization as well
as normalization. Several common transformations for achieving linearity are available, some of
which are listed as follows:
Feature Selection • 137
• cube root transformation
• hyperbolic transformation
• logarithmic transformation
• power transformation
• square root transformation
The following subsections briefly describe each transformation in the preceding bullet list.
Cube Root Transformation
Listing 5.4 displays the contents of that illustrates how to
cube_root_transformation.py
detect time gaps and display a summary of results.
LISTING 5.4: cube_root_transformation.py
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
#make this example reproducible
np.random.seed(0)
#create beta distributed random variable with 300 values
data = np.random.beta(a=4, b=15, size=300)
#create cube root transformed data
data_cbrt = np.cbrt(data)
#define grid of plots
fig, axs = plt.subplots(nrows=1, ncols=2)
#create histograms
axs[0].hist(data, edgecolor='black')
axs[1].hist(data_cbrt, edgecolor='black')
#add title to each histogram
axs[0].set_title('Original Data')
axs[1].set_title('Cube-Root-Transformed Data')
plt.show()
