WHAT IS FEATURE ENGINEERING?
Feature engineering requires creativity and domain knowledge. The goal is to improve
model performance while also ensuring that the features make sense in the context of the prob-
lem being solved.
Feature engineering is a crucial step in the process of building machine learning models.
It involves creating new features from the existing data or transforming the existing features to
improve the model’s performance. Feature engineering is important for several reasons, some
of which are listed as follows:
• improves model accuracy
• helps in understanding the underlying structure of the data
• can reduce the complexity of models
142 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
Feature engineering is a term that encompasses feature selection and feature extraction.
As you saw in Chapter 5, feature selection involves selecting a subset of existing features in a
dataset, whereas feature extraction involves creating new features that are based on a combi-
nation of existing features.
Moreover, feature extraction can be performed via a linear combination of features (such
as in Chapter 7) as well as a nonlinear combination of features. This chapter only discusses
PCA
algorithms that involve linear combinations of features. The following subsections provide
additional information regarding feature selection and feature extraction.
Types of Feature Engineering
The following bullet list contains different types of feature engineering:
• feature creation: deriving new features from existing ones
• feature transformation: normalization, scaling, or applying mathematical transformations
• feature selection: selecting a subset of the most important features
• feature extraction: reducing the dimensionality of the data, e.g., PCA
The following bullet list contains different techniques for feature engineering:
• binning: grouping values into bins
• one-hot encoding: converting categorical variables into a binary matrix
• polynomial features: creating interaction terms
• aggregation: summarizing multiple data points
• embedding: representing complex structures in simpler formats
The remainder of this section contains short code blocks that illustrate some of the
Python
techniques that are listed in the two previous bullet lists.
As you can undoubtedly surmise, feature creation involves a new feature based on existing
features. Here is a code block that illustrates a simple example of creating a new feature that is
based on two existing features in a data frame:
import pandas as pd
# Sample dataframe
df = pd.DataFrame({
'height': [5.5, 6.1, 5.8],
'weight': [150, 180, 160]
})
# Creating a BMI (Body Mass Index) feature
df['BMI'] = df['weight'] / (df['height']**2)
Feature transformation involves changing the distribution of the data in a dataset. One tech-
nique for doing so is called min-max scaling to scale a feature to a [0, 1] range. The following
snippet illustrates min-max scaling:
from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler
Feature Engineering • 143
scaler = MinMaxScaler()
df['height_scaled'] = scaler.fit_transform(df[['height']])
One-hot encoding involves converting a categorical feature into a binary matrix.
df = pd.DataFrame({'color': ['red', 'blue', 'green']})
df_encoded = pd.get_dummies(df, columns=['color'])
Binning is the process of grouping values into bins, an example of which is shown here:
df['height_bin'] = pd.cut(df['height'], bins=[5, 5.5, 6, 6.5],
labels=['short', 'average', 'tall'])
The next section summarizes a common sequence of feature engineering steps (among
others) that you need to perform in order to adequately prepare a dataset for a machine learn-
ing model.
WHAT STEPS ARE REQUIRED TO TRAIN A MODEL?
The following bullet list contains one fairly typical sequence of steps, starting from the data
preparation and through to the training of a model:
• data cleaning (in Chapter 3)
• data wrangling (in Chapter 4)
• imbalanced datasets
• feature scaling
• feature transformations
• feature selection (in Chapter 5)
• feature extraction (e.g., PCA in Chapter 7)
• hyperparameter tuning
• a different algorithm (optional)
• cloud-based training
The amount of time that is required for each step in the preceding bullet list depends heavily
on the nature of the data in the dataset, followed by the choice of machine learning algorithms.
If you have a dataset that is in pristine condition (i.e., no missing data, no incorrect data, and no
outliers), then the first two steps are essentially completed. However, if you have an imbalanced data-
set, consider downsampling or upsampling the dataset, or possibly a combination of both techniques.
Note that balanced data in a dataset is desirable for algorithms that use a distance metric,
because imbalanced data can cause algorithms to “favor” the class with the larger number of data
points. On the other hand, from a desirability perspective, we want everyone healthy in a cancer-
related dataset: as a result, we would prefer a highly imbalanced dataset in which all patients are
healthy. Thus, the balanced-versus-imbalanced dataset involves two opposite goals.
If you decide to use an algorithm that uses a distance-based metric (such as or ),
kNN kMeans
then you need to make sure that the data is properly scaled (as discussed in Chapter 5). In addition,
there is the scenario in which you apply a transformation (discussed in Chapter 5) to a dataset in
order to transform data so that it becomes normally distributed.
144 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
Another aspect to keep in mind is the number of features in a dataset. If the dataset contains
a very limited number of features, and it’s obvious which features you need to select for training a
model, then feature selection will probably work well. By contrast, if there are many features, or
the choice of significant features is not obvious, then consider performing dimensionality reduc-
tion (such as PCA) in order to work with a smaller number of features.
Finally, if the dataset is more than 500 gigabytes, you might encounter space-related issues
on a laptop that can be avoided by working in a cloud-based environment. Google Colaboratory
enables you to train models on a GPU for 12 consecutive hours per day, every day, and it’s free.
If need be, you can consider the USD 9.99/month which provides additional RAM and execution
time. Moreover, GCP offers a 2-terabyte storage option for USD 5 per month, and a 5-terabyte
storage option for USD 20 per month, which will probably accommodate most (if not all) of your
datasets. In addition to Google Colaboratory and GCP, there are other cloud-based solutions
available, so shop around to find the solution that works best for your needs.
Keep in mind that even if you perform all the preceding steps that you believe are necessary,
the trained model might not achieve the expected results on the test set. In this case, you can
attempt either (or both) of the following:
• hyperparameter tuning
• train on different algorithms
Hyperparameter tuning can be a time-consuming process, so you might be tempted to skip
the tuning step and select another algorithm. However, remember that there is no a priori assur-
ance that a different algorithm will produce better results: in fact, they might be worse.
MACHINE LEARNING AND ALGORITHM SELECTION
After you have cleaned a dataset, scaled the data, and performed transformations (if neces-
sary), you can select a machine learning algorithm to train a model on the dataset. A labeled
dataset is a dataset that contains a “target” column, which is a column that contains values that
identify each row in the dataset. Moreover, a labeled dataset enables you to select from various
classification algorithms, including:
• logistic regression
• naive Bayes
• decision trees
• random forests
• SVMs (support vector machines)
• CNNs (part of deep learning)
If you have unlabeled data, then you can explore one or more of the following clustering
algorithms (and other algorithms are also available):
kMeans
meanShift
kMeans++
kMedioid
DBScan
Feature Engineering • 145
Another consideration involves imbalanced datasets (discussed previously in this chapter),
which can adversely affect the accuracy of some machine learning algorithms as well as tech-
niques for handling imbalanced datasets, both of which are discussed in Chapter 5.
Training Large Datasets
If you have a dataset that is over 100MB (or whatever you consider to be “large”) and you
want to obtain results more quickly, these suggestions might save you a significant amount of
time.
First, perform your data cleaning steps on a 10% subset of the data. Second, use the smaller
dataset in the previous step with each of the algorithms that you plan to use. This approach will
give you faster results than the time for training the model just once on the entire dataset.
After completing the preceding sequence of steps, try randomly selecting yet another 10%
and randomly select one of the algorithms to train the model and see if the results are comparable.
Finally, train the model on the entire dataset with all the algorithms, and ideally the results
will be comparable. However, if there are differences then you could explore the data to deter-
mine if and where there might be something anomalous in the data.
Of course, avail yourself of the GPU in Google Colaboratory (or some other cloud-based
service), because it’s probably much faster and also free to use for at least 10 hours.
FEATURE IMPORTANCE
Before delving into the details of feature engineering, you probably want to know which fea-
tures in a dataset are significant and their relative importance. For example, the number of bed-
rooms and bathrooms in a house are significant features that affect the price of a house, whereas
the ticket number for an airplane ticket is most likely not a significant feature.
Suppose that a dataset has a “target” column, which is a column whose values we want to pre-
dict during the testing phase (i.e., after the training phase). A target column exists in datasets for
classification problems (which includes linear regression and classification algorithms), whereas
datasets without a target column are clustering tasks.
The key idea pertaining to feature importance involves assigning a numeric score between
0 and 100 that is assigned to each feature, which measures the extent to which that feature can
predict the target variable. The conditions for assigning feature importance are summarized
here:
• select all the relevant features
• assign a value between 0 and 100 to each feature
• ensure the sum of the feature values is 100
As a side note: if we divide the feature values by 100, we have a probability distribution.
One straightforward way to determine feature importance involves the random forest algo-
rithm in . For a given feature, calculates its importance (which is a numeric
Sklearn Sklearn
value) by examining the average reduction in impurity in the set of tree nodes that involve that
feature. This calculation is performed for each feature after the training step has completed,
after which the values are scaled (essentially a probability distribution) to simplify the compari-
son of the importance of features.

FEATURE ENGINEERING AND EXTRACTION
In addition to creating a dataset and “cleaning” its values, generally you will examine the
features in a dataset to determine which ones (if any) have little or no value and can be removed
from the dataset. The process for doing so involves three main techniques:
• feature engineering
• feature selection
• feature extraction (aka feature projection)
NOTE Feature selection is described in Chapter 5 and that material will not be repeated in this chapter.
Feature Engineering
Feature engineering is the process of either (a) selecting an existing subset of features (as
discussed in Chapter 5) or (b) determining a new set of features that are based on a combination
of existing features in order to create a meaningful dataset for a given task.
Domain expertise is often required for this process, even in the cases of relatively simple data-
sets. Feature engineering can be tedious and expensive, and in some cases, you might consider
using automated feature learning. After you have created a dataset, it’s a good idea to perform
feature selection or feature extraction (or both) to ensure that you have a high-quality dataset.
Feature Extraction
Feature extraction creates new features that are based on combinations of the original fea-
tures. By contrast, feature selection involves determining a subset of the existing features, which
is to say, no new features are created. The net effect of feature selection and feature extraction
results in dimensionality reduction for a given dataset (not discussed in this book).
Some of the advantages of feature extraction are listed here:
• Can capture interactions between features.
• New features might be more informative than the original ones.
• Often used in situations where the number of features is very high.
Some of the disadvantages of feature extraction are listed here:
• Transformed features often lose their original interpretable meaning.
• Not always straightforward to decide on the number of extracted features.
148 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
After you have ascertained the most important features in a dataset (and also cleaned
that dataset), the next step involves selecting a model, which is briefly discussed in the next
section.
Feature Extraction Algorithms
The following list contains various well-known feature extraction algorithms in machine
learning:
• principal component analysis (PCA)
• independent component analysis (ICA)
• linear discriminant analysis (LDA)
Note that the algorithms in the preceding list perform linear extraction, whereas the follow-
ing list involves nonlinear extraction/reduction algorithms:
• locally linear embedding (LLE)
• t-distributed stochastic neighbor embedding (t-SNE)
• UMap
In particular, t-SNE is capable of capturing complex nonlinear relationships in the data. This
algorithm uses Kullback-Leibler divergence (discussed in Chapter 7) to minimize the diver-
gence between two distributions: a distribution that measures pairwise similarities between the
input features, and a distribution that measures pairwise similarities between the points in the
low-dimensional space. This makes it particularly good at preserving local structure and can
reveal clusters in the data, making it popular for visualization purposes.
At the same time, keep in mind that there are some caveats regarding the t-SNE algorithm
(created by Geoffrey Hinton, who is often called the godfather of deep learning):
• high computational complexity
• careful tuning of hyperparameters
• random initialization can yield different results for each “run”
• difficulty in the interpretation of the reduced dimensions
Note that the t-SNE algorithm and the UMap algorithm are both discussed in more detail
in Chapter 7.
FEATURE HASHING
Feature hashing, also known as the hashing trick, is a technique used in machine learning
for dimensionality reduction and efficient handling of high-dimensional categorical features. It’s
particularly useful for large-scale and streaming data because it allows for a fixed-size represen-
tation of a potentially infinite feature space. Feature hashing involves the following:
• hash function
• fixed-size array
• collisions
Feature Engineering • 149
A hash function maps each unique feature name (or identifier) to an integer in a fixed range
[0,n-1], where n is the size of the hash table. Multiple feature identifiers may be mapped to the
same index due to collisions.
Features are hashed into a fixed-size array or “hash table.” The value at each position in the
array is the sum of the values of the features that hash to that position.
A collision occurs when different features hash to the same index, at which point a linked
list is populated with the feature value that resulted in a collision. If too many collisions occur,
then a larger fixed-size array is required: check for online articles that provide additional
details.
Advantages of feature hashing include the following:
• Memory Efficient: By using a fixed-size hash table, the memory footprint is kept low, espe-
cially useful for high-dimensional data.
• Computationally Efficient: The hashing operation is generally fast, making the technique
suitable for real-time and streaming applications.
• No Need for Dictionary: Unlike traditional methods that maintain a dictionary to map cat-
egorical variables to numerical identifiers, feature hashing does not require this.
Disadvantages of feature hashing include the following:
• Collisions: Features can collide, causing a loss of information. The likelihood of this can be
reduced by increasing the hash table size.
• No Inverse Mapping: Once features are hashed, you cannot revert them back to their origi-
nal form, which can make the model difficult to interpret.
• No Feature Importance: Since multiple features may be hashed to the same index, it’s hard
to interpret the importance of individual features.
Listing 6.2 displays the contents of that shows you how to perform
feature_hashing.py
feature hashing on a very simple dataset.
LISTING 6.2: feature_hashing.py
from sklearn.feature_extraction import FeatureHasher
# Sample data
data = [{'dog': 1, 'cat':2, 'elephant':4},{'dog': 2, 'run': 5}]
# Initialize FeatureHasher
hasher = FeatureHasher(n_features=4, input_type='dict')
# Transform the data
hashed_features = hasher.transform(data)
# The hashed features are now in a fixed-size array
print(hashed_features.toarray())

import data
very simple set of name/value pairs. The next code snippet initializes the variable as
hasher

an instance of the class , and then invokes the method with the
FeatureHasher transform()
contents of . The final code snippet displays the hashed features.

FEATURE SCALING AND ML ALGORITHMS
This section discusses types of scaling that you can perform, along with algorithms that
require feature scaling, followed by algorithms that do not benefit from feature scaling.
Selecting the Type of Scaling
Feature scaling is important in data preprocessing for various algorithms, some of which are
listed here:
• Lasso and Ridge penalties
• distance-based models
• k-NN (k nearest neighbor)
• clustering (kMeans)
• SVMs (support vector machines)
• ANNs (artificial neural networks)
The preceding algorithms perform better when the predictors have the same scale or are
within the same boundaries. As a general rule, select a particular type of scaling as follows:
• standardization or robust scaling if outliers exist
• standardization for Gaussian distributions
• normalization for non-normal distributions
Algorithms That Require Feature Scaling
Algorithms and models that depend on distance metrics are sensitive to feature scaling as
well as normalization, some of which are listed as follows:
• kMeans clustering
• kNN (k nearest neighbor)
• linear regression
• logistic regression
• NNs (neural networks)
• PCA (principle component analysis)
• SVMs (support vector machines)
Algorithms That Do Not Require Feature Scaling
Algorithms such as decision trees (for classification or for regression) and random forests
involve Gini impurity and entropy instead of distance metrics, so they are not sensitive to feature
Feature Engineering • 151
scaling. The following list contains some well-known machine learning algorithms that do not
require feature scaling:
• decision trees
• tree-based ensemble methods (e.g., random forest)
• naive Bayes
• graph-based algorithms
DATA SAMPLING TECHNIQUES
The following techniques are examples of well-known sampling data from a dataset:
• undersampling
• oversampling
• resampling
The techniques in the preceding bullet list are briefly described in the following subsections,
along with some of the trade-offs. Note that the previous chapter discussed SMOTE, which is
a powerful technique (originally based on the algorithm) for generating synthetic data in
kNN
order to achieve more balanced data in a dataset.
Undersampling
Undersampling refers to methods whose purpose is to balance the class distribution for a
classification dataset that has a skewed class distribution. These methods focus on a subset of
the larger class and are applied to a training dataset, after which a model can be trained on the
dataset.
Random undersampling also involves randomly selecting examples; however, the random
samples are taken from the majority class and then deleted from the training dataset, thereby
reducing the imbalance in the dataset.
The random undersampling technique removes samples from the dataset, and involves the
following:
• randomly remove samples from majority class
• can be performed with or without replacement
• alleviates imbalance in the dataset
• may increase the variance of the classifier
• may discard useful or important samples
However, random undersampling does not work so well with a dataset that has a 99%/1%
split into two classes. Moreover, undersampling can result in information loss that is useful for
a model.
Oversampling
Random oversampling focuses on randomly selecting (and replacing) examples from the
minority class in order to reduce the class imbalance in the training dataset. This technique
involves the following:
152 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
• oversample examples in the minority class
• duplicate examples from the minority class
• can balance distribution (no new information)
The second technique is better than the preceding technique, which involves the following:
• synthesize new examples from minority class
• a type of data augmentation for tabular data
• this technique can be very effective
• generate new samples from minority class
Another option is the package that provides various re-
Python imbalanced-learn
sampling techniques for datasets that exhibit class imbalance. This package is available in the
project. More details are here:
scikit-learn-contrib
https://github.com/scikit-learn-contrib/imbalanced-learn
Resampling
Random resampling transforms the training dataset into a new dataset, which is quite effec-
tive for imbalanced classification problems. Many techniques for handling imbalanced datasets
typically involve either generating more samples for the underrepresented class using (dis-
SMOTE
cussed in Chapter 5) or reducing the number of samples selected from the dominant class.
However, some datasets are fundamentally imbalanced due to the nature of the data in the data-
sets. For example, datasets contain far fewer people with cancer, diabetes, or other life-threatening
diseases than the number of healthy people in the dataset. Similarly, datasets containing credit
card fraud also have a very low percentage of fraud, and therefore are fundamentally imbalanced.
Unfortunately, oversampling the smaller class can lead to overfitting, and undersampling the
dominant class can lead to underfitting. Moreover, machine learning algorithms differ in terms of
their sensitivity to imbalanced datasets. For example, logistic regression is less sensitive to class
imbalance than a decision tree, whereas ensemble models are more impervious to overfitting.
DATA AUGMENTATION
In simple terms, data augmentation refers to adding more data to a dataset. Earlier you learned
about oversampling, which is a form of data augmentation. In general, the data augmentation tech-
niques that are available depend on the type of data that you want to augment, such as:
• image data
• tabular data
• NLP-related data
Well-known techniques for augmentation image data are typically 2D transforms, some of
which are listed here:
Feature Engineering • 153
• scaling images
• rotating images
• skewing images
Keep in mind that machine learning frameworks such as TensorFlow 2/Keras have built-in
support for 2D transforms.

file.
PNG
LISTING 6.3: image_augment1.py
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import skimage.io as io
from skimage.transform import rotate
filename = "sample3.png"
img = io.imread(filename)
def augment_img(img):
fig,ax = plt.subplots(nrows=1,ncols=5,figsize=(10,8))
# original image:
ax[0].imshow(img)
ax[0].axis('off')
# rotate image 45 degrees (counter clockwise):
ax[1].imshow(rotate(img, angle=45, mode = 'wrap'))
ax[1].axis('off')
# rotate image 45 degrees (counter clockwise):
ax[2].imshow(np.fliplr(img))
ax[2].axis('off')
# reflect image via horizontal axis:
ax[3].imshow(np.flipud(img))
ax[3].axis('off')
# rotate image 90 degrees (counter clockwise):
ax[4].imshow(np.rot90(img))
ax[4].axis('off')
# augment then display images:
augment_img(img)
plt.show()

COVARIANCE AND CORRELATION MATRICES
This section explains two important matrices: the covariance matrix and the correlation
matrix. Although these are relevant for PCA (principal component analysis), which is discussed
later, these matrices are not specific to PCA, which is the rationale for discussing them in a
separate section. If you are familiar with these matrices, feel free to skim through this section.
Covariance Matrix
As a reminder, the statistical quantity called the variance of a random variable is defined
X
as follows:
variance(x) = [SUM (x – xbar)*(x-xbar)]/n
156 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
A covariance matrix is a nxn matrix whose values on the main diagonal are the variance
C
of the variables , , . . . , . The other values of are the covariance values of each pair of
X1 X2 Xn C
variables and .
Xi Xj
The formula for the covariance of the variables and is a generalization of the variance of
X Y
a variable, and the formula is shown here:
covariance(X, Y) = [SUM (x – xbar)*(y-ybar)]/n
Notice that you can reverse the order of the product of terms (multiplication is commuta-
tive), and therefore the covariance matrix is a symmetric matrix:
C
covariance(X, Y) = covariance(Y,X)
Suppose that a CSV file contains four numeric features, all of which have been scaled appro-
priately, and let’s call them x1, x2, x3, and x4. Then the covariance matrix C is a 4x4 square matrix
that is defined as follows:
cov(x1,x1) cov(x1,x2) cov(x1,x3) cov(x1,x4)
cov(x2,x1) cov(x2,x2) cov(x2,x3) cov(x2,x4)
cov(x3,x1) cov(x3,x2) cov(x3,x3) cov(x3,x4)
cov(x4,x1) cov(x4,x2) cov(x4,x3) cov(x4,x4)
Note that the following is true for the diagonal entries in the preceding covariance matrix C:
var(x1,x1) = cov(x1,x1)
var(x2,x2) = cov(x2,x2)
var(x3,x3) = cov(x3,x3)
var(x4,x4) = cov(x4,x4)
In addition, C is a symmetric matrix, which is to say that the transpose of matrix C (rows
become columns and columns become rows) is identical to the matrix C. The latter is true
because (as you saw in the previous section) for any feature x and any
cov(x,y) = cov(y,x)
feature y.

The Correlation Matrix
As you learned in the preceding section, if the units of measure of features and do not
x y
have a similar scale, then the covariance matrix is adversely affected. The solution involves the
correlation matrix, which equals the covariance values divided by the standard devia-
cov(x,y)
tion and of and , respectively, as shown here:
stdx stdy x y
corr(x,y) = cov(x,y)/[stdx * stdy]
The correlation matrix no longer has units of measure, and we can use this matrix to find the
eigenvalues and eigenvectors.
Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors
According to a well-known theorem in mathematics (which you can find online), the eigen-
values of a symmetric matrix are real numbers. Consequently, the eigenvectors of C are vectors
in a Euclidean vector space (not a complex vector space).
In case you’re wondering, a nonzero vector x’ is an eigenvector of the matrix C if there is a
nonzero scalar lambda such that .
C*x' = lambda * x'
Now suppose that the eigenvalues of are , , , and , in decreasing numeric order

from left to right, and that the corresponding eigenvectors of are the vectors , , ,
C w1 w2 w3
and . These four vectors form the principal components of the new matrix.

Now that you understand how to calculate the covariance matrix and the correlation matrix,
you are ready for an example of calculating eigenvalues and eigenvectors, which are the topic of
the next section.

det

Notice that the eigenvectors [-1, 1] and [1,1] are orthogonal because their inner product is

[-1,1] * [1,1] = (-1)*1 + (1)*1 = 0
In fact, the set of eigenvectors of a square matrix (whose eigenvalues are real) are always
pairwise orthogonal, regardless of the dimensionality of the matrix.
Gauss Jordan Elimination (Optional)
This simple technique enables you to find the solution to systems of linear equations
“in place,” which involves a sequence of arithmetic operations to transform a given matrix to an
identity matrix.
Dimensionality Reduction • 159
The following example combines the Gauss-Jordan elimination technique (which finds the
solution to a set of linear equations) with the “bookkeeper’s method,” which determines the
inverse of an invertible matrix (its determinant is nonzero).
This technique involves two adjacent matrices: the left-side matrix is the initial matrix, and
the right-side matrix is an identity matrix. Next, perform various linear operations on the left-
side matrix to reduce it to an identity matrix: the matrix on the right side equals its inverse. For
example, consider the following pair of linear equations whose solution is x = 1 and y = 2:
2*x + 2*y = 6
4*x - 1*y = 2
Step 1: create a 2x2 matrix with the coefficients of x in column one and the coefficients of y
in column two, followed by the 2x2 identity matrix, and finally a column from the numbers on
the right of the equals sign:
| 2 2 | 1 0 | 6|
| 4 -1 | 0 1 | 2|
Step 2: add (-2) times the first row to the second row:
| 2 2 | 1 0 |6 |
| 0 -5 | -2 1 |-10|
Step 3: divide the second row by 5:
| 2 2 | 1 0 |6 |
| 0 -1 | -2/5 1/5 |-10/5|
Step 4: add 2 times the second row to the first row:
| 2 0 | 1/5 2/5 |2 |
| 0 -1 | -2/5 1/5 |-2|
Step 5: divide the first row by 2:
| 1 0 | -2/10 2/10 |1 |
| 0 -1 | -2/5 1/5 |-2|
Step 6: multiply the second row by (-1):
| 1 0 | -2/10 2/10 |1|
| 0 1 | 2/5 -1/5 |2|
As you can see, the left-side matrix is the 2x2 identity matrix, the right-side matrix is the
inverse of the original matrix, and the rightmost column is the solution to the original pair of
linear equations (x=1 and y=2).
PCA (PRINCIPAL COMPONENT ANALYSIS)
This section discusses PCA because it’s a very popular technique that you will encounter
frequently. Other techniques (such as SVD, also discussed later) are more efficient than PCA, so
it’s worthwhile to learn other dimensionality reduction techniques as well.
160 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
PCA is an unsupervised linear dimensionality reduction technique for transforming the fea-
ture space of a dataset into another feature space that has smaller dimensionality. Since PCA
involves a correlation matrix, which in turn involves variance, PCA is well-suited for features
that contain continuous data. Although you can use PCA for discrete data (such as binary data),
the results are less meaningful. A good discussion of PCA and discrete versus continuous data
is here:
https://stackoverflow.com/questions/40795141/pca-for-categorical-features
In particular, PCA involves linear algebra in order to calculate eigenvalues and eigenvectors
to linearly transform a feature space into another feature space that has lower dimensionality.
PCA constructs so-called “components,” each of which is a linear combination of features from
the initial dataset. Moreover, the components are pair-wise orthogonal, and they are the columns
of a matrix whereby the leftmost column is the most important component, and the rightmost
column is the least important component. Keep in mind the following points regarding the
PCA
algorithm:
• PCA is a variance-based algorithm
• PCA creates variables that are linear combinations of the original variables
• The new variables are all pair-wise orthogonal
• PCA can be a useful preprocessing step before clustering
• PCA is generally preferred for data reduction
can be useful for variables that are strongly correlated. If most of the coefficients in the
PCA
correlation matrix are smaller than 0.3, is not helpful. provides some advantages: less
PCA PCA
computation time for training a model (for example, using only five features instead of 100 fea-
tures), a simpler model, and the ability to render the data visually when two or three features are
selected. Here is a key point regarding :
PCA
PCA calculates the eigenvalues and the eigenvectors of the covariance (or correlation)
matrix C.
If you have four or five components, you won’t be able to display them visually, but you could
select subsets of three components for visualization, and perhaps gain some additional insight
into the dataset.
The algorithm involves the following sequence of steps:
PCA
1. Calculate the correlation matrix (from the covariance matrix) C of a dataset.
2. Find the eigenvalues of C.
3. Find the eigenvectors of C.
4. Construct a new matrix that comprises the eigenvectors.
The covariance matrix and correlation matrix were explained in a previous section. You also
saw the definition of eigenvalues and eigenvectors, along with an example of calculating eigen-
values and eigenvectors.
The eigenvectors are treated as column vectors that are placed adjacent to each other in
decreasing order (from left to right) with respect to their associated eigenvectors.
Dimensionality Reduction • 161
uses variance as a measure of information: the higher the variance, the more impor-
PCA
tant the component. In fact, just to jump ahead slightly: determines the eigenvalues and
PCA
eigenvectors of a covariance matrix (discussed in a previous section), and constructs a new
matrix whose columns are eigenvectors, ordered from left to right based on the maximum
eigenvalue in the leftmost column, decreasing until the rightmost eigenvector also has the
smallest eigenvalue.
Alternatively, there is an interesting theorem in linear algebra: if is a symmetric matrix, then
C
there is a diagonal matrix and an orthogonal matrix (the columns are pair-wise orthogonal,
D P
which means their pair-wise inner product is zero), such that the following holds:
C = P * D * Pt (where Pt is the transpose of matrix P)
In fact, the diagonal values of are eigenvalues, and the columns of are the corresponding
D P
eigenvectors of the matrix .
C
Fortunately, we can use and to calculate the mean, standard deviation, covari-
NumPy Pandas
ance matrix, correlation matrix, as well as the matrices and in order to determine the eigen-
D P
values and eigenvectors.
As an interesting point: any positive definite square matrix has real-valued eigenvectors,
which also applies to the covariance matrix because it is a real-valued symmetric matrix.
C
The New Matrix of Eigenvectors
The previous section described how the matrices and are determined. The leftmost
D P
eigenvector of has the largest eigenvalue, the next eigenvector has the second-largest eigen-
D
value, and so forth. This fact is very convenient: the eigenvector with the highest eigenvalue is
the principal component of the dataset. The eigenvector with the second-highest eigenvalue is
the second principal component, and so forth. You specify the number of principal components
that you want via the hyperparameter in the class of Sklearn.
n_components PCA
As a simple and minimalistic example, consider the following code block that uses for a
PCA
(somewhat contrived) dataset:
import numpy as np
from Sklearn.decomposition import PCA
data = np.array([[-1,-1], [-2,-1], [-3,-2], [1,1], [2,1], [3,2]])
pca = PCA(n_components=2)
pca.fit(X)
Note a trade-off here: we greatly reduce the number of components, which reduces the com-
putation time and the complexity of the model, but we also lose some accuracy. However, if the
unselected eigenvalues are small, we lose only a small amount of accuracy.

DIMENSIONALITY REDUCTION TECHNIQUES
In simplified terms, dimensionality reduction refers to reducing a high-dimensional vector
space to a lower dimensional vector space. There are several techniques that perform dimen-
sionality reduction (such as in the previous section), as listed here:
PCA
• PCA (principal component analysis)
• SVD (singular value decomposition)
• LLE (locally linear embedding)
• UMAP
• t-SNE
• PHATE
The previous section discussed because is a common algorithm for dimensionality
PCA PCA
reduction in machine learning. The other algorithms in the preceding list are discussed in a high-
level fashion in the following subsections.
Dimensionality reduction techniques belong to different categories, such as matrix factoriza-
tion ( and ), model-based reduction ( ), and manifold learning ( and ).
PCA SVD LDA SOM t-SNE
The Curse of Dimensionality
This term refers to the following phenomenon: as you increase the number of dimensions in
a dataset, the data points in the dataset tend to be farther apart, which in turn increases the pos-
sibility of overfitting. In a perfect world, the data points in a dataset are evenly distributed (how
wonderful this would be!), but in reality, different features have different distributions. One
feature might be almost constant, a second feature might have a Gaussian distribution, a third
feature might be bimodal, and so forth.
Nevertheless, many dimensionality reduction algorithms involve projecting data points in a
dataset to a lower-dimensional space (the other technique is discussed later).
What Are Manifolds (Optional)?
Although this is a concept in topology, which might seem wildly out of place in this book, it’s a
concept that is useful for understanding the second popular dimensionality reduction technique
called manifold learning.
As a simple example, walking on a sidewalk or driving a car feels like movement in two dimen-
sions (provided that you are not traversing a hill). In reality, you are moving along a sphere-like
object (the Earth), which means that you are moving in three dimensions as you walk on a non-
planar surface. However, the curvative of the non-planar surface so small that you don’t even
notice its effect, which is why this movement feels as though it’s happening along a plane.
In essence, small regions on a three-dimensional sphere are locally “similar” to a two-dimen-
sional plane (as described in the preceding paragraph). In general, a manifold is a topological
space that locally resembles Euclidean space for points that are “close” together. In mathemati-
cal terms, given any point of an n-dimensional manifold, there is a neighborhood of that point
that is homeomorphic to Euclidean space of the same dimensionality.
164 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
Some simple examples: lines and circles are one-manifolds but a “figure eight” is not a one-
manifold. Two-dimensional manifolds (also called surfaces) include planes, spheres, and the
torus (“donut”).
SVD (Singular Value Decomposition)
is a technique in linear algebra that factors any mxn matrix into the product of three
SVD A
matrices , , and , as shown here:
U S V
A = U*S*Vt
The preceding formula is true for any matrix (square, rectangular, invertible, or
A

U S V
•
U: (mxm) is orthogonal (U*Ut = I) with left singular vectors
•
V: (nxn) is orthogonal (V*Vt = I) with right singular vectors
•
S: (mxn) is diagonal with "singular values"
Recall that if , , and are compatible matrices, then the following transpose rules are true:
B C D
(B*C)t = Ct * Bt
(B*C*D)t = Dt * Ct * Bt
Now we can determine the nature of the matrices and by computing and , as follows:
U V At*A A*At
At*A = (V*S*Ut)*(U*S*Vt) = V*S^2*Vt (because Ut*U = I)
In fact, is the matrix of eigenvectors for , and is the matrix of eigenvalues for
V At*A S^2
. Now let’s compute , as follows:

A*At = (U*S*Vt)*(V*S*Ut) = U*S^2*Ut (because Vt*V = I)
Now U is the matrix of eigenvectors for , and is the matrix of eigenvalues for .

Since is a diagonal matrix of eigenvalues, the diagonal matrix consists of the square root
S^2 S
of the eigenvalues.

LLE (Locally Linear Embedding)
is a nonlinear dimensionality reduction technique that is based on the following two tasks:
LLE
1. compute the linear distance of a datapoint to its nearest neighbor, and then
2. attempt to determine a lower-dimensional representation of the data points such that the
local relationships are preserved.
Thus, the intuition of is similar to the intuition of . An example of using

t-SNE (“tee-snee”)
The (t-distributed stochastic neighbor embedding) algorithm is a nonlinear dimen-
t-SNE
sionality reduction technique in machine learning for visualization.
The goal of is to perform the following:
t-SNE
1. Map similar objects to nearby points.
2. Map dissimilar objects to more distant points.
The implementation of involves two probability distributions: one for pairs of high-
t-SNE
dimensional objects that perform #1, and another for pairs of low-dimensional objects that per-
form #2. The next step involves minimizing the KL (Kullback-Leibler) divergence for these two
probability distributions.
Note that is a much older technique (1930s) whereas was developed more recently
PCA t-SNE
(2008). performs linear dimension reduction that (a) maximizes variance and (b) preserves
PCA
166 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
large pairwise distances. As a result, different data points will be moved farther apart in ,
PCA
which might not work as well with nonlinear data.
By contrast, preserves small pairwise distances by means of an algorithm that calcu-
t-SNE
lates a similarity measure between data points in the high-dimensional space as well as in the
low-dimensional space. In addition, uses a cost function in order to optimize similarity
t-SNE
measures.
PHATE

PHATE " "
is a visualization and dimensionality reduction technique that does resemble : both
PHATE t-SNE
are sensitive to local and global relationships and attempt to preserve relationships among close
objects and distant objects.

LINEAR VERSUS NONLINEAR REDUCTION TECHNIQUES
One detail that has been glossed over is the difference between linear dimensionality reduc-
tion versus nonlinear dimensionality reduction algorithms. Before discussing those two types of
algorithms, a mathematical definition is required.
Suppose that is a mapping from vector space to the vector space (which can have differ-
T U V
ent dimensions), denoted as . Then is a linear transformation if the following are both
T:U->V T
true for any vectors and in and any real number :
u1 u2 U a
1.
T(u1+u2) = T(u1) + T(u2)
2.
T(a*u) = a*T(u)
For example, rotating a vector in the plane or scaling a vector in the plane is a linear transfor-
mation: shifting a vector in the plane is not a linear transformation (it’s an affine transformation).
If the dimensions of and V are the same and equal n, then a linear transformation can be
U T
represented as a nxn invertible matrix (i.e, it has an inverse). If the dimension of is lower than
V
the dimension of , then the linear transformation involves a projection. If the dimension of
U T V
is higher than the dimension of , then the transformation involves an embedding.
U T
For example, if doubles the size of the components of vectors in two-dimensional Euclidean
T

| 0 2 |

T T

Based on the definition of a linear transformation that you saw in the beginning of this sec-
tion, is a linear dimensionality reduction algorithm, whereas is a nonlinear
PCA kernel PCA
dimensionality reduction algorithm.
Linear reduction involves computing a linear combination of features or performing a linear
transformation. An example of the latter transforms a spanning set of vectors by a non-singular
matrix. Specifically, suppose that the set of vectors are orthogonal
B {v1, v2, . . . , vn}
and they form a basis for an n-dimensional vector space . In addition, suppose that is a nxn
V M
non-singular matrix (its determinant is non-zero). Then is a linear
{Mv1, Mv2, . . . , Mvn}
transformation of the basis that also spans the vector space V.
B
By contrast, nonlinear (NL) dimensionality reduction techniques perform nonlinear trans-
formations. However, in many cases, NL dimensionality reduction techniques are related to
linear methods, and they tend to belong to one of two groups:
1. NL techniques that provide high-to-low mapping (or vice versa)
2. NL techniques that provide visualization
Mapping methods are used to perform an initial feature extraction step, after which machine
learning algorithms can perform pattern recognition. On the other hand, NL techniques that
provide visualization perform calculations involving the distance between objects.
TYPES OF DISTANCE METRICS
Nonlinear dimensionality reduction techniques can also have different distance metrics.
For example, linear reduction techniques can use the Euclidean distance metric (based on the
Pythagorean theorem). However, you need to use a different distance metric to measure
the distance between two points on a sphere (or some other curved surface). In the case of NLP,
the cosine similarity metric is used to measure the distance between word embeddings (which
are vectors of floating-point numbers that represent words or tokens).
Distance metrics are used for measuring physical distances, and some well-known distance
metrics are listed here:
• Euclidean distance
• Manhattan distance
• Chebyshev distance
The Euclidean algorithm also obeys the triangle inequality, which states that for any trian-
gle in the Euclidean plane, the sum of the lengths of any pair of sides must be greater than the
length of the third side.
168 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
In spherical geometry, you can define the distance between two points as the arc of a great
circle that passes through the two points (always selecting the smaller of the two arcs when they
are different).
In addition to physical metrics, there are algorithms that implement the concept of “edit
distance” (the distance between strings), as listed here:
• Hamming distance
• Jaro–Winkler distance
• Lee distance
• Levenshtein distance
• Mahalanobis distance metric
• Wasserstein metric
The Mahalanobis metric is based on an interesting idea: given a point P and a probability dis-
tribution , this metric measures the number of standard deviations that separate point P from
D
distribution . More information about Mahalanobis is here:
D
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahalanobis_distance
In the branch of mathematics called topology, a metric space is a set for which distances
between all members of the set are defined. Various metrics are available (such as the Hausdorff
metric), depending on the type of topology.
The Wasserstein metric measures the distance between two probability distributions over a
metric space X. This metric is also called the “earth mover’s metric” for the following reason:
given two-unit piles of dirt, it’s the measure of the minimum cost of moving one pile on top of
the other pile.
KL Divergence bears some superficial resemblance to the Wasserstein metric. However,
there are some important differences between them. Specifically, the Wasserstein metric has
the following properties:
1. It is a metric.
2. It is symmetric.
3. It satisfies the triangle inequality.
On the other hand, KL divergence has the following properties:
1. It is not a metric (it’s a divergence).
2. It is not symmetric: KL(P,Q) != KL(Q,P).
3. It does not satisfy the triangle inequality.
Note that JS (Jenson-Shannon) divergence (which is based on KL divergence) is a true
metric, which would enable a more meaningful comparison with other metrics (such as the
Wasserstein metric).
Dimensionality Reduction • 169
WELL-KNOWN DISTANCE METRICS
There are several similarity metrics available, such as item similarity metrics, Jaccard (user-
based) similarity, and cosine similarity (which is used to compare vectors of numbers). The fol-
lowing subsections introduce you to these similarity metrics.
Another well-known distance metric is the so-called “taxicab” metric, which is also called the
Manhattan distance metric. Given two points A and B in a rectangular grid, the taxicab metric
calculates the distance between two points by counting the number of “blocks” that must be
traversed in order to reach B from A (the other direction has the same taxicab metric value). For
example, if you need to travel two blocks north and then three blocks east in a rectangular grid,
then the Manhattan distance is 5.
In fact, there are various other metrics available, which you can learn about by searching
Wikipedia. In the case of , the most frequently used distance metric is calculated via the
NLP
cosine similarity of two vectors, and it’s derived from the formula for the inner (“dot”) product
of two vectors.
Pearson Correlation Coefficient
Pearson similarity is the Pearson coefficient between two vectors. Given random variables
X and Y, and the following terms:
std(X) = standard deviation of X
std(Y) = standard deviation of Y
cov(X,Y) = covariance of X and Y
Then the Pearson correlation coefficient is defined as follows:
rho(X,Y)
cov(X,Y)
rho(X,Y) = -------------
std(X)*std(Y)
Keep in mind that the Pearson coefficient is limited to items of the same type. More informa-
tion about the Pearson correlation coefficient is here:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearson_correlation_coefficient
Jaccard Index (or Similarity)
The Jaccard similarity is based on the number of users which have rated item A and B divided
by the number of users who have rated either A or B. Jaccard similarity is based on unique words
in a sentence and is unaffected by duplicates, whereas cosine similarity is based on the length of
all word vectors (which changes when duplicates are added). The choice between cosine similar-
ity and Jaccard similarity depends on whether or not word duplicates are important.
The following method illustrates how to compute the Jaccard similarity of two sentences:
Python
def get_jaccard_sim(str1, str2):
set1 = set(str1.split())
170 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
set2 = set(str2.split())
set3 = set1.intersection(set2)
# (size of intersection) / (size of union):
return float(len(set3)) / (len(set1) + len(set2) - len(set3))
Jaccard similarity can be used in situations involving Boolean values, such as prod-
uct purchases (true/false), instead of numeric values. More information is available here:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaccard_index
Local Sensitivity Hashing (Optional)
If you are familiar with hash algorithms, you know that they are algorithms that create a hash
table that associates items with a value. The advantage of hash tables is that the lookup time to
determine whether or not an item exists in the hash table is constant. Of course, it’s possible for
two items to “collide,” which means that they both occupy the same bucket in the hash table.
In this case, a bucket can consist of a list of items that can be searched in more or less constant
time. If there are too many items in the same bucket, then a different hashing function can be
selected to reduce the number of collisions. The goal of a hash table is to minimize the number
of collisions.
The local sensitivity hashing (LSH) algorithm hashes similar input items into the same
“buckets.” In fact, the goal of LSH is to maximize the number of collisions, whereas traditional
hashing algorithms attempt to minimize the number of collisions.
Since similar items end up in the same buckets, LSH is useful for data clustering and near-
est neighbor searches. Moreover, LSH is a dimensionality reduction technique that places data
points of high dimensionality closer together in a lower-dimensional space, while simultaneously
preserving the relative distances between those data points.
More details about LSH are here:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locality-sensitive_hashing

# the contamination parameter defines the proportion of values

# predict on new data:
pred1 = model1.predict(new_data)
# negative values are outliers:
print("negative predicted values are outliers:")
print(pred1)
for i in range(new_data.shape[0]):
print("new_data[",i,"] = ",new_data[i],"is an outlier:",pred1[i]==-1)

WHAT IS BAYESIAN INFERENCE?
Bayesian inference is an important technique in statistics that involves statistical inference
and Bayes’ theorem to update the probability for a hypothesis as more information becomes
176 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
available. Bayesian inference is often called “Bayesian probability,” and it’s important in dynamic
analysis of sequential data.
Bayes’ Theorem
Given two sets A and B, let’s define the following numeric values (all of them are between
0 and 1):
P(A) = probability of being in set A
P(B) = probability of being in set B
P(Both) = probability of being in A intersect B
P(A|B) = probability of being in A (given you're in B)
P(B|A) = probability of being in B (given you're in A)
Then the following formulas are also true:
P(A|B) = P(Both)/P(B) (#1)
P(B|A) = P(Both)/P(A) (#2)
Multiply the preceding pair of equations by the term that appears in the denominator, and
we get these equations:
P(B)*P(A|B) = P(Both) (#3)
P(A)*P(B|A) = P(Both) (#4)
Now set the left side of equations #3 and #4 equal to each another and that gives us this
equation:
P(B)*P(A|B) = P(A)*P(B|A) (#5)
Divide both sides of #5 by P(B) and we get this well-known equation:
P(A|B) = P(A)*P(A|B)/P(B) (#6)
Some Bayesian Terminology
In the previous section, we derived the following relationship:
P(h|d) = (P(d|h) * P(h)) / P(d)
Each of the four terms in the preceding equation has a name.
First, the posterior probability is , which is the probability of hypothesis given the
P(h|d) h
data .
d
Second, is the probability of data given that the hypothesis was true.
P(d|h) d h
Third, the prior probability of is , which is the probability of hypothesis being true
h P(h) h
(regardless of the data).
Finally, is the probability of the data (regardless of the hypothesis).
P(d)
We are interested in calculating the posterior probability of P(h|d) from the prior probability
p(h) with P(D) and P(d|h).
Dimensionality Reduction • 177
What Is MAP?
The maximum a posteriori (MAP) hypothesis is the hypothesis with the highest probability,
which is the maximum probable hypothesis. This can be written as follows:
MAP(h) = max(P(h|d))
or:
MAP(h) = max((P(d|h) * P(h)) / P(d))
or:
MAP(h) = max(P(d|h) * P(h))
Why Use Bayes’ Theorem?
Bayes’ theorem describes the probability of an event based on the prior knowledge of the
conditions that might be related to the event. If we know the conditional probability, we can
use the Bayes rule to find out the reverse probabilities. The previous statement is the general
representation of the Bayes rule.
WHAT ARE VECTOR SPACES?
If you are already familiar with vector spaces, feel free to quickly skim through this section.
A vector space is a mathematical construct whose elements are vectors that typically consist
of a tuple of numbers. A real-valued vector space contains vectors of real numbers, whereas
a complex vector space contains vectors of complex numbers. The dimensionality of numeric
vector spaces is the minimum number of linearly independent vectors. For example, the vec-
tor u = [2,3] in the Euclidean plane is a real-valued two-dimensional vector, whereas the vector
v = [2+5i,4-7j] in the complex plane is a complex two-dimensional vector.
Every vector in a numeric vector space can be expressed as a linear combination of the vec-
tors on a basis. A spanning set is a superset of a basis, which means that some vectors in the
spanning set are a linear combination of the other vectors in the spanning set: those vectors are
dependent vectors. If the dependent vectors are removed from a spanning set, the remaining
vectors form a basis. As a side note: the vectors in a basis are not required to be unit vectors nor
must they be orthogonal to each other (examples shown later).
The real number line can be viewed as a one-dimensional vector space. The vector i = [1,0]
is a unit vector that forms a so-called basis for this vector space because any other vector is a
multiple of the vector i.
The Euclidean plane can be viewed as a two-dimensional vector space. The unit vectors
i = [1,0] and j = [0,1] are unit vectors that form a basis for this vector space because any other
vector is a linear combination of the vectors i and j. Notice that the dot product of i and j is 0,
which means that they are orthogonal. However, the vectors in the set {2*i, 3*j} are not unit
vectors, and yet they form a basis for the Euclidean plane.
178 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
As another example, the vectors in the set {i+j, i} also form a basis, even though they are not
orthogonal and one of them is not a unit vector. However, any basis can be transformed into any
other basis for a given vector space, which means that any basis can be transformed into a basis
that consists of orthonormal unit vectors (search online for proof of this statement).
In the three-dimensional Euclidean space, the set of vectors {i,j,k} form an orthonormal
basis, where the unit vectors i, j, and k are [1,0,0], [0,1,0], and [0,0,1], respectively.
In general, an n-dimensional vector space V has a basic S that consists of n orthonormal vec-
tors. If you create a matrix M whose rows are the vectors in a basis, then the inverse of matrix M
exists because the rows are linearly independent.
In fact, a set of n linear equations in n unknowns has a unique solution if (and only if) the
associated matrix M is invertible. Statistical packages contain one or more algorithms for solving
systems of linear equations.
SUMMARY
This chapter started with an introduction to covariance matrices and correlation matrices.
Next, you learned about eigenvalues and eigenvectors, and how to calculate them using the
Gauss Jordan Elimination algorithm.
Then you learned about the Principal Component Analysis (PCA) algorithm, which followed
by other well-known techniques for dimensionality reduction techniques, such as SVD (Singular
Value Decomposition), LLE (Locally Linear Embedding), UMAP, t-SNE, and PHATE.
In addition, you learned about distance metrics and some well-known distance metrics, such
as Pearson Correlation Coefficient, Jaccard Index, and Local Sensitivity Hashing. Finally, you
learned about the machine learning library Scikit-Learn, Bayesian Inference, and then an intro-
duction to vector spaces.

This appendix introduces you to the command, which is a highly versatile utility for

regular expressions and metacharacters in the same way other Unix commands do. This lets you
plug it into other expressions and do almost anything, at the cost of adding complexity to a com-
mand string that may already be doing quite a lot already. It is almost always worthwhile to add a
comment when using awk; it is so versatile that it won’t be clear which of the many features you
are using at a glance.

The (Aho, Weinberger, and Kernighan) command has a C-like syntax and you can use

How Does the awk Command Work?
The command reads the input files one record at a time (by default, one record is one
awk
line). If a record matches a pattern, then an action is performed (otherwise no action is per-
formed). If the search pattern is not given, then performs the given actions for each record
awk
of the input. The default behavior if no action is given is to print all the records that match the
given pattern. Finally, empty braces without any action does nothing; that is, it will not per-
form the default printing operation. Note that each statement in actions should be delimited by
semicolons.
In other to make the preceding paragraph more concrete, here are some simple examples
involving text strings and the command (the results are displayed after each code snippet).
awk
The switch sets the field separator to whatever follows it, in this case a space. Switches will
-F
often provide a shortcut to an action that normally needs a command inside a block:
BEGIN{}
x="a b c d e"
echo $x |awk -F" " '{print $1}'

a

a b c d e

c a

LISTING A.20: AddSubtract1.sh
myFile="mixednumbers.txt"
awk -F"|" '
BEGIN { line = 0; total = 0 }
{
split($1, arr, "-")
f1 = arr[1]
if($1 ~ /-/) { f1 = -f1 }
line += f1
split($2, arr, "-")
f2 = arr[1]
if($2 ~ /-/) { f2 = -f2 }
line += f2
split($3, arr, "-")
f3 = arr[1]
if($3 ~ /-/) { f3 = -f3 }
line += f3
printf("f1: %d f2: %d f3: %d line: %d\n",f1,f2,f3, line)
total += line
line = 0
}
END { print "Total: ",total }
' $myfile

f1: -453 f2: 30 f3: -298 line: -721
f1: -783 f2: 20 f3: -347 line: -1110
Total: -3128
The code assumes we know the format of the file. The split function turns each field record
into a length two vector, first position = number, second position either an empty value or a
dash, and then captures the first position number into a variable. The if statement just sees if the
original field has a dash in it. If the field has a dash, then the numeric variable is made negative,
otherwise it is left alone. Then it adds the line up.

The function gives you a random number. The values of are uniformly dis-

tributed between 0 and 1: the value is never 0 and never 1.
Often you want random integers instead. Here is a user-defined function you can use to

function randint(n) {
return int(n * rand())
}
The product produces a random real number greater than 0 and less than n. We then make
it an integer (using int) between 0 and n - 1.
Here is an example where a similar function is used to produce random integers between 1
and n:
awk '
# Function to roll a simulated die.
function roll(n) { return 1 + int(rand() * n) }
# Roll 3 six-sided dice and print total number of points.
{
printf("%d points\n", roll(6)+roll(6)+roll(6))
}'
Note that starts generating numbers from the same point (or “seed”) each time is
rand awk
invoked. Hence, a program will produce the same results each time it is launched. If you want a
program to do different things each time it is used, you must change the seed to a value that will
be different in each run.
Use the function to set the starting point, or seed, for generating random numbers
srand(x)
to the value . Each seed value leads to a particular sequence of “random” numbers. Thus, if
x
you set the seed to the same value a second time, you will get the same sequence of “random”
numbers again. If you omit the argument x, as in , then the current date and time of
srand()
day are used for a seed. This is how to obtain random numbers that are truly unpredictable. The
return value of is the previous seed. This makes it easy to keep track of the seeds for use
srand()
in consistently reproducing sequences of random numbers.
The function (not in all versions of ) returns the current time in seconds since
time() awk
January 1, 1970. The function (not in all versions of ) takes a numeric argument in
ctime awk
seconds and returns a string representing the corresponding date, suitable for printing or further
processing.
194 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
The function gives you the positive square root of . It reports an error if x is
sqrt(x) x
negative. Thus, is 2.
sqrt(4)
awk 'BEGIN{
print sqrt(16);
print sqrt(0);
print sqrt(-12);
}'
The output is here:
4
0
nan
ONE-LINE AWK COMMANDS
The code snippets in this section reference the text file , which you can populate
short1.txt
with any data of your choice.
The following code snippet prints each line preceded by the number of fields in each line:
awk '{print NF ":" $0}' short1.txt
Print the rightmost field in each line:
awk '{print $NF}' short1.txt
Print the lines that contain more than two fields:
awk '{if(NF > 2) print }' short1.txt
Print the value of the rightmost field if the current line contains more than two fields:
awk '{if(NF > 2) print $NF }' short1.txt
Remove leading and trailing whitespaces:
echo " a b c " | awk '{gsub(/^[ \t]+|[ \t]+$/,"");print}'
Print the first and third fields in reverse order for the lines that contain at least three fields:
awk '{if(NF > 2) print $3, $1}' short1.txt
Print the lines that contain the string one:
awk '{if(/one/) print }' *txt
As you can see from the preceding code snippets, it’s easy to extract information or subsets
of rows and columns from text files using simple conditional logic and built-in variables in the
command.
awk

LISTING A.30: mixed-data.sh
#-----------------------------------------
# 1) remove blank lines
# 2) remove line feeds
# 3) print a LF after every fourth field
# 4) remove trailing ',' from each row
#-----------------------------------------
inputfile="mixed-data.txt"
outputfile="aligned-data.txt"
grep -v "^$" $inputfile |awk -F"," '{printf("%s",$0)}' | awk '
BEGIN { columnCount = 4 }
{
for(i=1; i<=NF; i++) {
printf("%s ", $i)
if( i % columnCount == 0) { print "" }
}
}' > temp-columns
# 4) remove trailing ',' from output:
cat temp-columns | sed 's/, $//' | sed 's/ $//' > $outputfile
Listing A.30 starts with a command that removes blank lines, followed by an com-
grep awk
mand that prints the rows of the dataset as a single line of text. The second command initial-
awk
izes the variable with the value 4 in the block, followed by a loop that
columnCount BEGIN for
200 • Python 3 and Feature Engineering
iterates through the input fields. After four fields are printed on the same output line, a linefeed
is printed, which has the effect of realigning the input dataset as an output dataset consisting of
rows that have four fields. The output from Listing A.30 is here:
Sara, Jones, 1000, CA
Sally, Smith, 2000, IL
Dave, Jones, 3000, FL

Working With awk • 201
{

LISTING A.47: string2date2.sh
inputfile="dates2.csv"
outputfile="formatteddates2.csv"
rm -f $outputfile; touch $outputfile
for line in 'cat $inputfile'
do
fname='echo $line |cut -d"," -f1'
lname='echo $line |cut -d"," -f2'
date1='echo $line |cut -d"," -f3'
# convert to new date format
newdate='echo $date1 | awk '{ print substr($0,1,4)"-"substr($0,5,2)"-
"substr($0,7,2)" "substr($0,9,2)":"substr($0,11,2)":"substr($0,13,2)}''
# append newly formatted row to output file
echo "${newdate},${fname},${lname}" >> $outputfile
done

SUMMARY
This appendix introduced the command, which is essentially an entire programming
awk
language packaged into a single Unix command.
We explored some of its built-in variables as well as conditional logic, while loops, and
for
loops in in order to manipulate the rows and columns in datasets. You then saw how to
awk
delete lines and merge lines in datasets, and also how to print the contents of a file as a single
line of text. Next you learned how to use meta characters and character sets in commands.
awk
You learned how to perform numeric calculations (such as addition, subtraction, multiplication,
and division) in files containing numeric data, and also some numeric functions that are available
in .
awk
In addition, you saw how to align columns in a dataset, how to delete columns, how to select
a subset of columns from a dataset, and how to work with multiline records in datasets. Finally,
you saw a couple of simple use cases involving nested quotes and date formats in a structured
dataset.
At this point you have all the tools necessary to do quite sophisticated data cleansing and
processing, and it is strongly encouraged that you try to apply them on some task or problem of
interest. The final step of the learning process is doing something real.
“I saw something similar once, I wonder if there is a way to . . .?” or the even more common
“how do I do XXX in language YYY?” You can’t ask those questions if you don’t have a sense of
what is possible.
At this point there is one more thing to say: congratulations! You have completed a fast-paced
yet dense book, and if you are a bash neophyte, the material will probably keep you busy for
many hours. The combined effect demonstrates that the universe of possibilities is larger than
the examples in this book, and ultimately they will spark ideas in you. Good luck!
